15 Trajectory Fine-Tuning

Purpose

Agent stack focus for this chapter.

Why can training on a useful recovery record teach the model to repeat the mistake instead of the correction?

While prompt engineering and runtime guardrails guide an off-the-shelf model, fine-tuning modifies the neural network weights directly, embedding specialized decision policies and tool calling conventions into model parameters. However, training on multi-turn trajectories requires strict separation between agent proposals and external environment observations: for trajectory imitation, loss applies only to selected policy responses, while environment feedback remains conditioning context. Naive fine-tuning can teach a policy to hallucinate its own tool responses or overfit to static workspace layouts, degrading general reasoning and error recovery. Systems engineering governs how datasets are partitioned, how loss masks are applied, and how updated checkpoints are validated against regression suites before deployment. An update is worth releasing only when closed-loop task acceptance improves under the same runtime that still bounds every proposal the model makes, however far its training loss has fallen.

Learning Objectives
  • Assign agent behavior to weights, context, or runtime, and decide when a failure warrants a weight change
  • Construct model inputs that preserve intended conditioning and price per-invocation serialization in training tokens
  • Map admitted response spans to causal token targets, observation masks, and termination policy
  • Compare loss weighting and construct batches without unintended cross-example dependencies
  • Calculate imitation regret bounds on reference and learner states to decide when on-policy labeling pays
  • Calculate preference margins and gradient weights, and distinguish what a comparison measures from task acceptance
  • Select behavior sources and parameter artifacts using explicit resource and version accounting
  • Evaluate a versioned policy for release on closed-loop acceptance, cost per accepted task, and schema transfer, while preserving runtime enforcement

15.1 Recovery Records as Training Data

Fine-tuning reshapes each model call but leaves every exposure to the runtime.

Consider a recovery demonstration admitted by the curation pipeline for the repair agent (lighthouse 1.1) into repair_demonstrations_v1. During the recorded session, the agent generated a malformed edit proposal that the runtime validator refused prior to tool dispatch. In response to the failure notification, the model inspected the tool schema, generated a corrected edit, and produced candidate patch ws_r20. From this multi-turn trajectory, the target-selection policy recovery_targets_v1 marks three assistant turns as supervised targets: the schema inspection request (e3), the corrected edit (e4), and the scoped final summary (e6). The malformed proposal (e2) and the runtime’s refusal remain in the input history as conditioning context. The member that entered the corpus is the redacted view trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, and the corpus manifest binds it to those role assignments, its lineage, and its split membership.

An admitted record is not yet in a form a training loop can safely ingest. The raw event log is an interleaved transcript of heterogeneous events, recording model generations, runtime observations, and tool responses in the exact sequence they occurred. If a data loader treats every assistant message as a supervised target, it computes loss on the malformed edit proposal alongside the correction, directly reinforcing the initial mistake. If the training pipeline computes loss across all sequence tokens indiscriminately, the model expends parameter capacity predicting deterministic environment replies, such as compiler diagnostics or file directory listings, that the model will never emit during inference. If the batching pipeline packs multiple trajectories into a shared context window without attention masking, self-attention heads attend across sample boundaries, leaking private workspace contents into unrelated training examples. Standard cross-entropy loss fails silently under each of these conditions. The empirical loss decreases steadily as the optimizer fits the data, concealing severe behavioral corruption from offline training metrics. While curation contracts determine which actions demonstrate desirable behavior and evaluation contracts measure the deployed agent, neither specifies how to convert a multi-turn execution trace into isolated, causal gradient updates.

The path from an admitted record to an evaluated policy update begins with a question curation leaves open, namely which parts of the repair behavior belong in weights at all (section 15.2). The answer fixes what the data pipeline must protect. Serialization reconstructs each model input exactly as the agent received it (section 15.3), loss masking places the gradient on the selected responses and nowhere else (section 15.4), and batching shares buffers without letting examples read one another or skew one another’s weight (section 15.5). Masking and batching guard directly against the three silent failures above, and serialization guards the premise both rely on, that each prefix is the one the model actually saw.

A faithful loader still trains on recorded prefixes, and the deployed policy acts on prefixes its own choices produce. Exposure bias prices that gap over the horizon and decides when labeling the learner’s own states pays (section 15.6), and schema robustness asks the same question of the tool interfaces the policy is shown (section 15.7). The remaining sections vary the update itself along three independent choices: the objective, where preference optimization contrasts valid alternatives that imitation cannot rank (section 15.8); the behavior source, where distillation moves a costlier configuration’s behavior into a cheaper one (section 15.9); and the parameter artifact, where adapters change what a run must store (section 15.10). Closed-loop evaluation decides whether any of them earned a release (section 15.11), and a training manifest for the repair policy assembles the whole chain (section 15.12).

15.2 Scope of Supervised Fine-Tuning

Modifying model weights is an expensive, high-blast-radius intervention reserved for specific failure modes. The intervention ladder orders changes by cost and reversibility, so engineers consider cheaper prompt, schema, and runtime mitigations before committing compute to parameter updates, while diagnostic evidence can justify a direct weight update. Supervised fine-tuning can address systematic behavioral defects rooted in the policy itself rather than omitted instructions or missing runtime constraints. The principle of verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) requires curation to relate the recorded evidence to a declared learning use and distinguish admissible targets from contextual or rejected actions. While this ordering serves as a defensive default justified by operational cost and blast radius, section 15.9 examines the complementary scenario where fine-tuning proves more economical than paying the recurring token latency and cost of carrying lengthy guidance in every prompt.

↩︎ The intervention ladder, which orders context, schema, runtime, and parameter interventions by cost and reversibility, is established in The intervention ladder.

Supervised fine-tuning (SFT) adjusts trainable model parameters to maximize the conditional likelihood of selected target tokens given their causal prefix context. When processing an admitted trajectory record such as trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, the training loop must implement the specific recovery sequence defined in recovery_targets_v1 rather than indiscriminately computing loss over every recorded token. The model must observe the historical context and tool outputs that justify a chosen action, yet the data pipeline must strictly censor subsequent execution steps that would prematurely reveal future observations or solutions. Trajectory fine-tuning therefore inherits two distinct data contracts: a target-selection contract that designates which tokens contribute to the loss gradient, and a conditioning contract that governs what causal history the model observes when generating those tokens.

This distinction defines what it means for an agent to learn improved behavior rather than overfit an idiosyncratic run. In an error-recovery trajectory, the true behavioral goal is teaching the model to inspect an updated schema after encountering a refusal, issue a properly formed corrective call, and interpret what the runtime verification check establishes. Merely memorizing the file paths of a specific test fixture or predicting hard-coded test outputs minimizes training loss without acquiring generalizable recovery skills. Evaluating whether the updated policy transfers these procedural habits to unfamiliar environments and novel tasks is precisely what section 15.11 measures.

15.2.1 Procedure, contracts, and enforcement

Every response generated by an agent emerges from two inputs: the model’s static weights and the dynamic context window assembled for that invocation. Because fine-tuning updates only the weights, system architects must divide agent capabilities into three architectural homes, summarized in table 1:

  • Procedure represents behavior that remains stable across tasks and evolves slowly, such as inspecting diagnostic output before modifying source code or auditing unresolved issues before reporting task completion. Procedure is what model weights can reliably internalize.
  • Contracts represent the dynamic operational facts that vary across deployments, user sessions, and individual execution turns, such as API tool definitions, schema requirements, environment parameters, and granted permissions. Contracts belong strictly in the prompt context; a model that has memorized an earlier schema will continue emitting obsolete arguments when an interface changes.
  • Enforcement represents the critical invariants that must hold even when the model generates incorrect or adversarial proposals, such as schema validation, authorization checks, sandbox containment, and execution timeouts. Enforcement must reside in the deterministic runtime harness under invariant closure (principle 1).

Relying on parameters to store interface contracts creates immediate operational brittleness. When a tool interface adds a required parameter or alters its return structure, a model that relies on memorized signatures will continue generating obsolete calls. Similarly, a revoked authorization remains revoked regardless of how frequently the action appeared in successful training demonstrations. Fine-tuning cannot replace dynamic contracts; what it can teach is the procedural discipline of consulting and adhering to whichever contract the context window specifies.

This architectural boundary disproves the naive view that fine-tuning simply moves the prompt into the weights. Only the procedural components of a prompt (structured reasoning guidelines, diagnostic checklists, or few-shot error recovery examples) can be absorbed into the parameters, saving substantial token overhead on every turn (section 15.9). The contractual components must remain in the prompt context, because omitting them forces the model to guess the operational facts of the current execution environment. Furthermore, the deterministic checks that enforce authorization and boundary invariants cannot reside in either prompts or weights, because probabilistic token distributions cannot guarantee invariant execution.

Fine-tuning alters the policy’s proposal distribution: which tools it requests, how thoroughly it investigates diagnostic evidence, and which execution paths it explores. These behavioral shifts directly influence task success rates, end-to-end latency, and token consumption. Nevertheless, every proposed action executes entirely within the execution horizons, state machines, and authority boundaries established by the runtime harness. This separation is the proposal boundary. Training reshapes what the policy proposes, while the runtime decides which actions are permitted to execute. This principle governs every adaptation strategy in this chapter, culminating in the release verification criteria.

15.2.2 Objective, behavior source, and artifact

Designing an adaptation pipeline requires making three orthogonal decisions:

  • Training Objective: The loss formulation that defines the learning signal. Supervised imitation learning maximizes the likelihood of curated target demonstrations, whereas preference learning techniques such as direct preference optimization (section 15.8) optimize policy weights by contrasting preferred trajectories against dispreferred alternatives under an explicit ranking criterion.
  • Behavior Source: The origin of the demonstrations and environmental conditioning. Traces may be harvested from instrumented production systems and filtered through offline verification, generated by a teacher configuration with privileged context or an expanded search budget (section 15.9), labeled on the states the learner itself reaches, or synthesized by injecting faults the recordings never contained (section 15.6.2).
  • Parameter Artifact: The physical representation of the updated weights. Teams can fine-tune all model parameters directly or train modular adapter matrices, such as low-rank adaptation (LoRA) modules, that attach to a frozen base checkpoint (section 15.10).

Because these three design dimensions are orthogonal, they combine modularly: a deployment might train a lightweight parameter adapter using a direct preference objective derived from synthetic teacher demonstrations.

Regardless of which combination an architecture adopts, the training process depends fundamentally on how trajectory records are converted into model inputs. Serialization determines tokenization and visibility windows, loss masking isolates the precise prediction targets, and batching schedules balance gradient variance across variable-length interaction turns. When deployed, the trained artifact will inevitably encounter unseen environmental states and revised tool schemas, requiring empirical validation before release. Constructing the prefix input and conditioning window for each targeted response constitutes the initial systems hurdle.

15.3 Serializing Trajectories

Consider the repair demonstration admitted during curation: it contains a malformed edit proposal, a runtime refusal, a schema inspection, and a corrected edit. A training loader that strips out the refusal fundamentally alters the problem the subsequent correction answered. Similarly, a loader that places a later passing test check before the edit leaks future evidence that the model never possessed when proposing the patch. While curation identifies which model completions are worthy of reinforcement, serialization must reconstruct the exact prompt and observation history that elicited them. This includes preserving any runtime redactions, such as synthetic placeholders substituted for private file paths, so the training example faithfully reflects the environment the model actually encountered.

15.3.1 Reconstructing each model input

Runtime event logs record system state transitions, but an event log alone does not guarantee what was present in a model’s context window. System prompts evolve, tool schemas update dynamically, and intermediate messages are often summarized or truncated to fit token budgets. To reconstruct inputs accurately, the training pipeline relies on delivered-input manifests. These manifests identify the exact messages, tool definitions, summaries, and bounded observations dispatched to the model, along with their contents and schema versions. The manifest provides the authoritative record that a specific payload entered an invocation, preventing accidental data leakage or historical revisionism.

↩︎ The delivered-input manifest that records the exact payload sent to the model, rather than mutable paths or references, is established in The log event schema.

When constructing an example that demands historical fidelity, serialize the selected invocation directly from this recorded manifest. The example must preserve the observation exactly as the agent saw it, including partial buffers, truncated outputs, or execution errors, rather than substituting a clean or complete result obtained later in the run. Conversely, when intentionally transforming an example, apply the reviewed transformation systematically to both the prompt and the associated response, retain parent trace references, and recheck schema compatibility. Shortening prompts for context distillation (section 15.9) is one such transformation.

Table 2 outlines how curated responses map to their valid conditioning context in the running repair demonstration. While the table highlights the core causal dependencies, the fully rendered training example incorporates the entire context available at that turn, including system instructions and active tool definitions.

Notice the causal boundaries across these turns. The refusal at e2 demonstrates that runtime validation caught the malformed proposal before dispatch; no tool executed on the host. The check summary at e5 arrived only after the agent issued the corrected edit, so it can condition the final report at e6, but cannot appear in the context for e4. Similarly, selecting the schema inspection at e3 isolates the model’s query tokens for training while treating the returned schema as external observation. Maintaining these explicit associations ensures that subsequent loss masking isolates model decisions from environment observations.

15.3.2 Rendering with versioned templates

Before training begins, structured message objects (system instructions, user prompts, assistant thoughts, tool calls, and environment returns) must be serialized into the token sequence expected by the target model. Chat templates differ substantially across model architectures. Even checkpoints derived from the same base model often disagree on how to format tool schemas, delimit parallel calls, or signal message boundaries. A serialized dataset artifact must therefore record the tokenizer version, template configuration, tool-formatting schema, and response-boundary policy alongside the token arrays. The true model input consists of the message sequence paired with this concrete rendering configuration.

In schematic form, a multi-turn tool-calling example follows the structure shown below. The delimiter strings are illustrative placeholders, as each model family defines its own special tokens. Here, the system segment defines the task contract and tool definitions, the assistant segment emits an optional reasoning trace followed by two parallel tool calls with unique identifiers, and each tool segment returns an observation matched to its corresponding call identifier.

<|system|> task contract; schemas for read_file(target_uri), run_tests(target) <|end|>
<|user|> Fix the connection-pool leak in src/pool.py <|end|>
<|assistant|> <|reasoning|> Reproduce the failure, then read the module. <|end_reasoning|>
  <|call id=c1|> {"name": "read_file", "arguments": {"target_uri": "src/pool.py"}} <|end_call|>
  <|call id=c2|> {"name": "run_tests", "arguments": {"target": "tests/test_pool.py"}} <|end_call|>
<|end_turn|>
<|tool id=c2|> FAILED test_release: pool exhausted <|end|>
<|tool id=c1|> ...file contents... <|end|>
<|assistant|> ...

In this sequence, c2 completes before c1, and this runtime delivers observations in arrival order, so the listing places the c2 reply first because that is the prompt the model actually received at its next turn. The call identifiers keep each observation bound to its original invocation regardless of that order. The error to avoid is re-sorting the replies into call order (c1, then c2) for tidiness, which would invent a prompt the model never saw. Serialization assembles tokens according to how the runtime presented the prompt to the model, whatever order that was. Reordering calls whose arguments or observations depend on one another alters the causal problem; any such permutation constitutes a synthetic data transformation that requires explicit validation. The <|end_turn|> token records where the policy surrendered control to the runtime loop, while all tokens enclosed between <|tool|> and <|end|> constitute external observations.

The training pipeline bundles this rendered dataset as serialization_v1. Its manifest couples the admitted execution records with the active rendering configuration, mapping every selected response span to its exact token offsets. Because tokenization, template injection, and string redaction can shift token boundaries (leading whitespace can merge into an adjacent special token, for example), the pipeline must re-verify all token offsets against the final rendered array. A rigorous pipeline decodes completed examples to inspect task prompts, tool bindings, span boundaries, and end-of-sequence markers. If an example fails boundary alignment or drops content, the pipeline drops the record entirely rather than patching it with synthetic filler.

Role delimiters establish protocol syntax, but they convey no runtime authority. If a model generates text that mimics a <|tool|> delimiter, that text remains model output under the tokenizer’s grammar; the serialization pipeline must never promote unverified generations into genuine environment observations.

↩︎ The rule that model output is a proposal carrying no authority until the runtime validates it is established in The Model Invocation Boundary.

15.3.3 One example per invocation

Serving runtimes vary in how they manage conversational history. Some systems preserve full transcripts across iterations, while others prune earlier steps, compact intermediate context, or discard chain-of-thought scratchpads between turns to reduce KV cache footprints. A single concatenated transcript provides a faithful training example only when its causal attention prefixes match what the model actually perceived during online inference. The repair agent’s own runtime compacts history once the working set crosses its watermark, so a turn delivered after compaction saw a digest, not the raw turns that a concatenated transcript would place before it. When serving prunes or compresses history between steps, each invocation must instead be serialized as an independent training example containing its exact delivered prefix.

↩︎ Watermark-triggered compaction, which replaces earlier turns with a digest between invocations, is established in Context Compaction.

This independence incurs a substantial computational penalty, and its arithmetic is the triangular sum that already priced an append-only trajectory at serving time (equation 2.9), now paid in stored training tokens. If a trajectory spans \(\TurnHorizon\) turns and each turn contributes roughly \(\TurnAppendLength\) tokens, the example for turn \(\TurnIndex\) spans approximately \(\TurnIndex\,\TurnAppendLength\) tokens. Serializing all \(\TurnHorizon\) invocations independently requires \(\TurnAppendLength\,\TurnHorizon(\TurnHorizon+1)/2\) tokens, compared to just \(\TurnHorizon\,\TurnAppendLength\) tokens for a unified transcript, an expansion factor of \((\TurnHorizon+1)/2\). Over a stipulated horizon of 30 turns, serializing each invocation separately multiplies the required token volume by 15.5×. An agent system’s history-retention policy is therefore a primary engineering constraint when sizing training compute. The same fidelity rule covers the model’s own reasoning text. If serving discards an earlier turn’s rationale before the next invocation, the reconstructed prefix for that invocation omits it too; whether retained rationale then becomes a target or stays context is a separate choice (section 15.4.4).

With serialization_v1 assembled, the pipeline defines the tokenized sequences, their conditioning evidence, and the response spans selected during curation. The remaining challenge is determining which token predictions contribute to the training loss. Unmasked cross-entropy over entire multi-turn sequences would inadvertently penalize the model for failing to predict external tool outputs or user messages. Observation loss masking converts the curated span boundaries into aligned prediction targets while preserving necessary observations as conditioning context.

15.4 Observation Loss Masking

A data loader that naively selects every assistant token trains the model on malformed proposals alongside their subsequent corrections, unraveling careful curation decisions. Worse, a loader that computes loss across all tokens asks the policy to predict external environment responses. Serialization structures the input sequence into tokens, but the loss mask determines which of those tokens generate parameter updates.

15.4.1 The masked objective

Let \(\SerializedTrainingExample=(\SequenceToken_1,\ldots,\SequenceToken_{\SequenceLength})\) be one serialized example, with an initial context or boundary token at \(\SequenceToken_1\). Let \(\EligibleTargetPositionSet\subseteq\{2,\ldots,\SequenceLength\}\) contain the eligible policy-token positions selected for imitation. Position eligibility depends on admitted response spans and the specific training objective rather than token authorship alone. The mask is \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=\IndicatorFunction\{\TokenIndex\in\EligibleTargetPositionSet\}\), with \(\PositionLossMask_1=0\). For an example with at least one selected target, the mean masked cross-entropy is

\[\MaskedFinetuningLoss(\ModelParameters)=-\frac{1}{\ActiveTargetTokenCount}\sum_{t=2}^{\SequenceLength}\PositionLossMask_{t}\log\Policy(\SequenceToken_t\mid \TokenPrefix),\qquad \ActiveTargetTokenCount=\sum_{t=2}^{\SequenceLength}\PositionLossMask_{t}>0. \tag{1}\]

Each selected token is predicted from the preceding prefix. During training, the model conditions on the recorded sequence prefix, including earlier actions, observations, and admitted mistakes, rather than sampling fresh rollouts at each step. This training regime is teacher forcing1 (Williams and Zipser 1989). It allows recovery prefixes that contain historical failures to serve as valid conditioning contexts, provided the trajectory satisfies the curation contract.

1 Teacher Forcing: Williams and Zipser named the technique in 1989 for recurrent networks, feeding the recorded target back as the next input instead of the network’s own output. Because every prefix is recorded rather than generated, a causal model computes all of an example’s next-token predictions in one parallel forward pass, which is why imitating a trajectory costs one pass per example rather than one sampled rollout per decision.

In the running repair trajectory, target_mask_v1 maps reviewed response spans directly onto the tokens emitted by serialization_v1, distinguishing target tokens from conditioning context (table 3). Modifying a tokenizer vocabulary, adjusting chat formatting templates, or transforming text inputs inevitably alters token alignments. Consequently, the pipeline must regenerate and validate masks against the exact serialization output rather than copying offsets across pipeline stages.

When an example contains no selected targets (\(\ActiveTargetTokenCount=0\)), the loss in equation 1 is undefined. The data loader must either drop such examples from the supervised objective or process them under an explicit fallback policy; it must never record them as completed demonstrations. Batch-level weighting strategies across heterogeneous examples present distinct numerical trade-offs, developed in section 15.5.

Definition 15.1: Observation loss masking

Observation loss masking excludes supplied observations from the direct token-prediction loss while retaining the permitted observation context needed to train selected policy responses.

  1. Significance: Focuses the optimization objective strictly on the policy’s conditional distribution \(\Policy(\TrajectoryAction_{t}\mid \TrajectoryState_{t})\). Consequently, verbose environment output cannot dilute the gradient signal for policy actions, regardless of how much sequence context observations consume (notebook 15.1 quantifies this dilution).
  2. Distinction: Masked tokens remain in the input context and participate in self-attention; only their next-token loss terms are omitted. Furthermore, policy authorship alone does not qualify a token as a target: rejected attempts or toxic actions require masking even when generated by an assistant role.
  3. Common pitfall: Selecting an entire multi-turn tool exchange simply because the tool call succeeded, inadvertently training the model to predict external environment responses.

15.4.2 Causal label alignment

In causal autoregressive models,2 the hidden state at input index \(\TokenIndex-1\) generates the logits that predict token \(\SequenceToken_t\). The data loader must therefore align the prediction logits at \(\TokenIndex-1\) with target label \(\SequenceToken_t\) and target mask \(\PositionLossMask_{t}\), rather than attaching the mask of input token \(\SequenceToken_{t-1}\). Table 4 illustrates this boundary alignment. Because several distributed training frameworks perform this causal shift internally within their loss functions, applying a manual shift inside the data loader can silently misalign targets by one token across the entire corpus. Inspect the aligned labels and predicted tokens on a synthetic short example before launching large-scale training runs.

2 Causal Self-Attention: Causal masking in Transformer decoders restricts each token from attending to subsequent positions (Vaswani et al. 2017). This triangular attention structure allows a single forward pass over an input sequence of length \(\SequenceLength\) to compute all next-token predictions in parallel. It also enforces the label shift: the network predicts \(\SequenceToken_t\) using only the representation formed at \(\TokenIndex-1\).

Vaswani, Ashish, Noam Shazeer, Niki Parmar, Jakob Uszkoreit, Llion Jones, Aidan N. Gomez, Lukasz Kaiser, and Illia Polosukhin. 2017. “Attention Is All You Need.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 30: 5998–6008.

Let \(\VocabularyTokenLogit_{t-1,\VocabularyToken}\) denote the logit for vocabulary token \(\VocabularyToken\) produced from \(\TokenPrefix\), using an ordinary softmax and no label smoothing. Differentiating equation 1 gives

\[\frac{\partial\MaskedFinetuningLoss}{\partial \VocabularyTokenLogit_{t-1,\VocabularyToken}}=\frac{\PositionLossMask_{t}}{\ActiveTargetTokenCount}\left(\Policy(\VocabularyToken\mid \TokenPrefix)-\IndicatorFunction\{\VocabularyToken=\SequenceToken_t\}\right).\]

When \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\), the next-token prediction at position \(\TokenIndex-1\) yields zero direct loss derivative. The token nevertheless shapes subsequent representations through causal attention layers, allowing gradients backpropagated from downstream active losses to adjust the parameters that encode it. The model conditions on tool outputs without expending capacity attempting to synthesize them. This mechanism underpins verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) at token-level precision.

A deployed agent trajectory records an interleaved dialogue between two distinct systems: the agent policy and the external environment. Training without observation masking forces a single parameter set to approximate two conflicting distributions simultaneously:

  1. The agent policy \(\Policy(\TrajectoryAction_{t}\mid \TrajectoryState_{t})\), mapping historical observations and instructions to deliberate tool calls or answers.
  2. An environment dynamics model \(\EnvironmentTransitionDistribution(\TrajectoryState_{t+1}\mid \TrajectoryState_{t}, \TrajectoryAction_{t})\), predicting outputs returned by arbitrary shells, database engines, or remote APIs.

Because real tools execute directly during deployment and supply ground-truth outputs, tasking the policy with predicting environment returns wastes model capacity.

Supervising the environment distribution introduces two severe systems failures:

  1. Loss dilution: Under standard token-averaged cross-entropy, unmasking inflates the normalization denominator from \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\) to the number of causal prediction terms, \(\SequenceLength-1\), because the initial stored boundary has no preceding-position prediction. As a result, the effective gradient scale on critical decision tokens collapses proportionally to the selected fraction of predicted positions (computed in notebook 15.1). The loss budget then goes to runtime artifacts (file paths, stack traces, compiler dumps, and raw JSON payloads), which pull on the average in two directions. Repetitive parts such as echoed schemas, boilerplate headers, and repeated paths are easy to predict and drag the averaged loss down; novel parts such as a fresh stack trace or unseen file contents are hard to predict and swamp the gradient. Either way, the averaged number stops measuring the policy.
  2. Role confusion: When trained to predict tokens following a tool invocation, the policy learns that tool outputs naturally succeed tool calls within the same autoregressive stream. In production, such a model can continue past its own tool call and write a fabricated tool result instead of ending the turn with the stop reason that hands control back to the runtime. Because the runtime separates generated text from authenticated environment observations (section 15.3), it discards the fabricated result, so every such turn burns output budget on tokens that are thrown away and can stall the trajectory until the runtime intervenes.

The full repair record selects three responses (e3, e4, and e6). In the simplified view of figure 1, which leaves out the schema request e3 and its returned schema, only two of the seven segments shown, the corrected tool call and the final response, carry an active mask (\(\PositionLossMask_{t}=1\)) and receive direct imitation loss. The prompt, task statement, refused proposal, rejection message, and tool result carry \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\) and remain conditioning context. Gradients from later selected targets still update the parameters that encode them.

Observation masking eliminates both pathologies at the loss level. A validation loss curve cannot reveal how often an unmasked model fabricates environment outputs in practice; that failure mode emerges only during interactive, closed-loop evaluation. While predicting environment transitions is a legitimate goal when training world models or offline tool simulators, it conflicts with the policy imitation objective required for autonomous agents.

15.4.3 Selected targets and compute

Observations fill 77.1 percent of the input; the loss sits on 4.9 percent.

The count of selected positions \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\) establishes the denominator of the loss function and dictates the gradient magnitude assigned to each target token. In agent workflows, extensive tool outputs frequently consume the vast majority of sequence memory while serving as conditioning context for a concise corrective action. Comparing selected targets against the number of causal prediction terms reveals the exact dilution factor an unmasked objective would impose; their share of stored input instead measures occupancy. Notebook 15.1 quantifies these allocations across two alternative target policies evaluated on the same trajectory.

Napkin Math 15.1: Stored tokens and selected targets
Problem: A hypothetical serialized example has the segment counts in table 5. Its curation record excludes some policy-response positions from imitation while retaining them as context. If the optional rationale is either selected or kept as context only, how many positions receive direct loss, what fraction of the stored input do they occupy, and by how much would an unmasked loss dilute each of them?

Variables: Exactly 160 of the other-policy-response positions belong to the refused proposal and stay context; every other response position is selected. The rationale is reviewed for either target policy, both policies see the same input, the initial position belongs to the prompt, and there is no padding.

Math: The disjoint segments sum to 16,384 stored tokens, of which observations occupy 77.1 percent. Removing the refused positions leaves 960 \(-\) 160 \(=\) 800 selected response targets, and selecting the rationale adds 1,400 more. The first stored boundary has no preceding-position prediction, so an unmasked causal average divides by 16,383 prediction terms instead of \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\). Each selected target’s coefficient therefore shrinks by 16,383/800, a factor of 20.5× (7.4× when the rationale is selected). Under each target policy, table 6 sets \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount\) against the stored input it is drawn from.

Result: Both masks place the whole loss on a small minority of stored positions and leave the context identical; they differ only in whether the rationale is a target.

Systems insight: Because the dilution factor scales inversely with the selected fraction of predicted positions, it expands rapidly as environment interactions accumulate. Doubling the observation volume while holding response lengths constant escalates dilution from 20.5× to 36.3×. Consequently, observation masking becomes increasingly essential as agents tackle deep, tool-intensive reasoning traces. Notice, however, that masking yields negligible reductions in FLOPs: every masked token must still pass through causal attention layers to form context for subsequent predictions. Only the final language model head projection and cross-entropy loss computation can be bypassed at masked positions.

Recorded repair trajectories show that the hypothetical inventory’s proportions are realistic. Figure 2 splits the transcripts of five published agent configurations on the same repository-repair tasks (Jimenez et al. 2024) by the role that produced each message.

Across the 5 configurations and 2,100 runs, observations make up 56 to 72 percent of the transcript, and model output only 12 to 31 percent. These shares count each message once. Serializing one example per invocation repeats the growing prefix in every example, so the observation share of the stored training input is larger still, approaching the inventory’s proportion. The mask is therefore not a refinement applied to a few verbose tool calls; in recorded repair work it decides the fate of most of the sequence.

15.4.4 Optional rationale as target or context

Many agent datasets incorporate intermediate reasoning traces, such as chain-of-thought scratchpads or tool-use plans. Deciding how to supervise this intermediate text requires choosing between three distinct data-loading regimes (table 7). Supervising the rationale trains the model to generate its own reasoning before invoking tools. Retaining the rationale under a loss mask treats the reasoning as supplied conditioning context, training the model to act upon plans provided by an external orchestrator or user. Stripping the rationale entirely fundamentally alters the conditional distribution by forcing the policy to act directly from context alone.

Formally, given context prefix \(\TurnContext_{t}\), rationale tokens \(\RationaleTokens\), and action tokens \(\ResponseActionTokens=(\ResponseActionTokens_1,\ldots,\ResponseActionTokens_n)\), response-only supervision models the conditional distribution \(\Policy(\ResponseActionTokens_j\mid \TurnContext_{t}, \RationaleTokens, \ActionTokenPrefix)\). If the production serving environment does not provide \(\RationaleTokens\), the deployed model must evaluate \(\Policy(\ResponseActionTokens_j\mid \TurnContext_{t}, \ActionTokenPrefix)\). Because these represent mathematically distinct conditional distributions, omitting rationale at training time represents an explicit distillation experiment that must be validated directly against the unconditioned serving target.

At inference time, an external planning agent or the policy’s own autoregressive generator can supply the required reasoning text. When the model generates its own rationale, runtime text inevitably diverges from the training demonstration; downstream evaluation must measure this divergence. A rationale segment merits target status only when the system architecture explicitly requires the deployed policy to synthesize its own thoughts under operational latency and token budgets.

15.4.5 Termination and truncated responses

To teach an agent when to yield control, the training loss must include the authentic policy-generated termination token (such as <|eom_id|> or <|im_end|>) at the end of each selected response. Host frameworks often insert their own control delimiters to demarcate incoming tool payloads; data loaders must never confuse these host delimiters with genuine policy stop tokens. Excluding termination tokens from the loss mask deprives the model of direct supervision on when to stop. Without that supervision, the policy can generate past the end of its action until the output limit cuts it off. Such calls return TRUNCATED rather than COMPLETED, so the runtime’s quarantining rule withholds a proposal that may have been correct.

↩︎ The normalized TRUNCATED status and the quarantining rule that withholds an incomplete candidate are established in The normalized status envelope.

Truncated responses, where an output was cut short by a context window or a timeout, present a dangerous edge case. A common pipeline flaw is appending a synthetic end-of-turn token to an incomplete response, which trains the policy to end a turn on a partial command. That is the training-side twin of the hazard the quarantining rule blocks at serving time, where a truncated rm -rf argument still parses as a complete and broader command. The training-side version is worse, because a policy that has learned to emit the terminator after a partial command produces a COMPLETED status, which the quarantining rule admits. When encountering truncated turns, engineering pipelines should adopt one of two sound strategies:

  1. Conservative exclusion: Mask the truncated turn entirely (\(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\)) while preserving preceding successful turns in the trajectory.
  2. Prefix-only supervision: Supervise the valid initial tokens of the truncated turn up to the truncation point, but strictly omit any terminating token.

Whenever trajectories undergo truncation, sliding-window packing, or template transformations, the data pipeline must recompute target masks and verify that \(\ActiveTargetTokenCount > 0\). Robust training loaders implement automated unit assertions on critical boundaries: the first token of an agent response, the closing turn terminator, the transition into tool observations, rejected recovery attempts, and truncated sequences. Validating these boundaries guarantees that the training engine optimizes the exact supervised objective declared by the curation policy.

Checkpoint 15.1: Defend the target mask

With per-example loss masking in place, the training loader enforces the exact boundary between conditioning context and imitation targets. Production training, however, batches heterogeneous trajectories across varying sequence lengths and device topologies. The batching and collation mechanisms must uphold this target contract without silently skewing loss weights across sequence lengths.

15.5 Batching Variable-Length Trajectories

Consider a batch where a lengthy debugging and code repair trace shares a single training buffer with a brief, unrelated file lookup. Under an ordinary lower-triangular causal mask, the lookup can attend directly to the repair trace’s intermediate file contents and tool outputs, context that the lookup policy will never encounter during online inference. Under token averaging, the multi-turn repair trace will also outweigh the brief lookup by the exact ratio of their active target tokens.

Constructing a batch of variable-length trajectories therefore forces two coupled systems decisions: how much gradient weight each trajectory exerts on the optimization objective, and how multiple sequences share physical buffer memory without leaking information across sequence boundaries. Sequence packing is strictly a memory layout optimization; a correct implementation preserves both the mathematically specified loss and the causal isolation of each sequence’s predictive context.

15.5.1 Loss normalization across variable-length examples

Let a batch contain \(\BatchSize\) examples. Example \(\BatchExampleIndex\) has stored length \(\SequenceLength^{(\BatchExampleIndex)}\), including context, and \({\ActiveTargetTokenCount}_{\BatchExampleIndex}>0\) selected target tokens under the preceding section’s mask. Let \(\LossFunction_{\BatchExampleIndex}\) be its mean masked negative log-likelihood. Two batch objectives are

\[\TokenAveragedBatchLoss= \sum_{\BatchExampleIndex=1}^{\BatchSize}\frac{{\ActiveTargetTokenCount}_{\BatchExampleIndex}}{\sum_{k=1}^{\BatchSize}{\ActiveTargetTokenCount}_k}\LossFunction_{\BatchExampleIndex}, \tag{2}\]

\[\ExampleAveragedBatchLoss=\frac{1}{\BatchSize}\sum_{\BatchExampleIndex=1}^{\BatchSize}\LossFunction_{\BatchExampleIndex}. \tag{3}\]

Token averaging in equation 2 assigns equal coefficients to selected token losses. Example averaging in equation 3 assigns equal coefficients to example means. Both count selected targets, never context or padding. A record with no selected targets supplies no \(\LossFunction_{\BatchExampleIndex}\) and is excluded from either objective.

These normalization coefficients govern the loss function directly, though realized gradient magnitudes also depend on prediction errors and parameter sensitivities. Example averaging equalizes serialized example means, not completed interactions or underlying tasks; one example may represent a single invocation. In contrast, token averaging weights each example proportionally to its active target count.

This distinction becomes critical when trajectory serialization splits a multi-turn interaction into separate training records. If one of \(\BatchSize\) initial examples becomes \(\TurnHorizon\) per-invocation examples while every other example remains once, example averaging changes that trajectory’s aggregate share from \(1/\BatchSize\) to \(\TurnHorizon/(\BatchSize-1+\TurnHorizon)\). It has \(\TurnHorizon\) times each unsplit example’s share, rather than \(\TurnHorizon\) times its former normalized share. Under token averaging, the trajectory’s aggregate share remains unchanged only if its selected targets form a disjoint partition, each turn is selected once, and the other batch members and their targets stay unchanged. Repeating prefix context alone does not repeat active targets. Data sampling, trajectory segmentation, and loss normalization therefore jointly dictate how underlying agent capabilities are weighted during optimization. Writing out the coefficients each objective places on an example mean and on a single selected token (table 8) makes the difference exact.

The mathematical equivalence in table 8 requires careful distributed accounting. A large batch is rarely processed in one piece. It is split into microbatches (slices processed one after another before a single parameter update) and spread across data-parallel workers (each holding a copy of the model and processing its own share of the batch). Computing a true global token average requires summing active token losses and token counts across all microbatches and workers before dividing. Averaging local microbatch means directly produces an accurate token average only when active token counts match across all microbatches; otherwise, the workers must combine their raw sums and counts before taking the ratio.

15.5.2 Example isolation in packed buffers

Definition 15.2: Sequence packing

Sequence packing places several variable-length training examples in shared buffers while preserving their intended information boundaries and loss targets.

  1. Significance: Uniform padding allocates the longest example’s length to every row, reducing batch occupancy to the ratio of mean to maximum sequence length. Sequence packing reclaims this wasted capacity (notebook 15.2).
  2. Distinction: Independent trajectories share physical memory buffers without sharing predictive context; multi-turn interactions within an individual trajectory retain access to their complete history.
  3. Common pitfall: Applying a standard causal mask across concatenated sequences, allowing later examples to attend illegitimately to earlier ones.

The file lookup from the section’s opening must never read the repair trace’s file contents or tool observations. Reading them would be cross-contamination,3 the leak a packed layout must prevent (Krell et al. 2021). Let \(\QueryBufferPosition\) and \(\KeyBufferPosition\) denote buffer positions, \(\QueryVector_i\) and \(\KeyVector_j\) their query and key vectors, and \(\AttentionHeadDimension\) the attention-head dimension. The pre-softmax attention scores \(\text{score}_{i,j}\) enforce both causality and example membership:

3 Cross-Contamination: Krell and colleagues used the term for attention and label leakage between sequences packed into one buffer, and showed that masks, position resets, and per-sequence loss handling make a packed model mathematically equivalent to the unpacked one. The equivalence is the property to test: a packed loader that trains faster but lets examples read one another has changed the learning problem, and no loss curve will show it.

\[\text{score}_{i,j}=\begin{cases} \QueryVector_i\KeyVector_j^T/\sqrt{\AttentionHeadDimension} & \text{if }\operatorname{example}(\QueryBufferPosition)=\operatorname{example}(\KeyBufferPosition)\text{ and }\KeyBufferPosition\le \QueryBufferPosition,\\ -\infty & \text{otherwise}. \end{cases} \tag{4}\]

Under an ordinary causal mask, every query in a later example can score keys in an earlier one; the block-diagonal mask sets each of those cross-example entries to \(-\infty\) before the softmax, and excludes trailing padding keys the same way (figure 3). Variable-length attention kernels (Dao 2023) enforce this without materializing a dense \(-\infty\) matrix. The loader hands the kernel the example boundaries as cumulative offsets, \(0\), \(\PackedExampleLength_1\), \(\PackedExampleLength_1+\PackedExampleLength_2\), and so on through the buffer’s total length (\(\BatchSize+1\) entries for \(\BatchSize\) examples), so that example \(\PackedExampleIndex\) occupies one contiguous interval of positions, and the kernel computes scores only for query and key pairs that fall inside the same interval. Whether a given kernel also skips the computation for cross-example blocks of the score matrix, and how much time that saves, is an implementation property to measure on the target accelerator. The mask is the correctness requirement; skipped work is a throughput bonus that must be shown, not assumed.

Dao, Tri. 2023. “FlashAttention-2: Faster Attention with Better Parallelism and Work Partitioning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2307.08691.

One long trajectory holds padded occupancy to 19.1 percent; packing reaches 76.6 percent.

Loss computation requires the same rigorous boundary isolation. In autoregressive language modeling, targets are created by shifting labels by one position. Shifting labels across an entire concatenated buffer inadvertently trains the final token of one example to predict the initial token of the next. Each example’s initial prompt context remains unselected as a target, and shifted prediction-target pairs must stay strictly inside their parent example. Attention masks and target masks enforce distinct systems constraints: attention masks isolate visible context, while target masks isolate supervised loss gradients. Both must be implemented correctly.

Napkin Math 15.2: Stored occupancy and target weighting
Problem: A batch holds one long trajectory among short ones. How much allocated sequence space does packing save over uniform padding, and how differently do the two loss normalizations weight a long and a short example?

Variables: Eight hypothetical examples have stored lengths \([32{,}768,\ 8{,}192,\ 4{,}096,\ 2{,}048,\ 1{,}024,\ 1{,}024,\ 512,\ 512]\). Each packed buffer holds \(32{,}768\) positions. Separately, two examples have \(256\) and \(8{,}192\) selected targets.

Math: Padding eight rows to the maximum allocates \(8\times32{,}768\) positions, or 262,144, for 50,176 stored tokens. Packing places the longest example alone in one buffer and the remaining 17,408 tokens in a second buffer. The two buffers allocate \(2\times32{,}768\) positions, or 65,536. Dividing stored tokens by allocated positions gives an occupancy of 19.1 percent with padding (the mean length of 6,272 divided by the maximum of 32,768) and 76.6 percent with packing. Packing allocates 4× fewer positions.

For the separate target-count illustration, token averaging gives coefficients \(256/(256+8{,}192)\) and \(8{,}192/(256+8{,}192)\), or 3.03 percent and 96.97 percent on the example means. Their ratio, \(8{,}192/256\), is 32×. Example averaging instead assigns each mean \(1/2\).

Systems insight: Padded occupancy is the ratio of mean to maximum length, so the single longest trajectory sets the waste: one 32,768-token example holds this batch to 19.1 percent, and packing raises it to 76.6 percent. Occupancy counts context and targets together, so the throughput it buys is measured on the actual attention kernel. Normalization is a separate knob with an equally large effect. Token averaging gives the long example 32× the short one’s weight, and example averaging gives them equal weight. Choose token averaging when every selected token should count equally, and example averaging when a few long trajectories should not dominate the update.

15.5.3 Position conventions and equivalence checks

Masks settle what each position may read, but each token also carries a position index, and a packed example sits at some offset inside its buffer. The loader must therefore give each example the positions it would have had alone, or show that the offset makes no difference to its outputs. The direct way is to reset positions to zero at each example boundary. For zero-based buffer offsets \(\PackedExampleOffset_k\) and \(\PackedExampleOffset_{k+1}\) bounding example \(\PackedExampleIndex\),

\[\operatorname{pos}[\TokenIndex]=\TokenIndex-\PackedExampleOffset_k\quad\text{for }\TokenIndex\in[\PackedExampleOffset_k,\PackedExampleOffset_{k+1}).\]

Rotary position embeddings (RoPE), a widely used scheme that encodes position by rotating query and key vectors through position-dependent angles, are the case where the offset provably makes no difference, so for them resetting is an operational convention rather than a mathematical requirement. In the fixed-frequency formulation, position \(\QueryBufferPosition\) applies a rotation \(\RotaryRotationMatrix_i\) to a query or key vector. Shifting both positions by a common offset \(\CommonPositionShift\) leaves their relative rotation unchanged:

\[\RotaryRotationMatrix_{i+\CommonPositionShift}^T\RotaryRotationMatrix_{j+\CommonPositionShift}=\RotaryRotationMatrix_{j-i}=\RotaryRotationMatrix_i^T\RotaryRotationMatrix_j.\]

A common offset therefore leaves within-example attention scores unchanged, a direct consequence of the relative-position construction of RoPE (Su et al. 2024). Absolute-position features, position-dependent frequency scaling, and floating-point non-associativity can still compromise equivalence for a particular architecture, so no position scheme is exempt from checking.

Su, Jianlin, Murtadha Ahmed, Yu Lu, Shengfeng Pan, Wen Bo, and Yunfeng Liu. 2024. “RoFormer: Enhanced Transformer with Rotary Position Embedding.” Neurocomputing 568: 127063. https://doi.org/10.1016/j.neucom.2023.127063.

The general answer is therefore a test rather than a proof. A rigorous correctness test compares selected-token logits and the evaluated loss for the same examples processed separately and packed, with stochastic layers disabled and numerical tolerances explicitly stated. Perturbing one example must leave every other example’s logits unchanged. This test exercises attention boundaries, label shifting, position IDs, and the chosen denominator together. When it passes, the loader trains exactly the demonstrated problem. The trained policy, however, acts in contexts its own choices produce.

15.6 Exposure Bias

Suppose a repair policy trained on this corpus is placed in a fresh bounded trial and meets a version conflict instead of the malformed-argument rejection recorded in trace_r19_01_redacted_v1. A plausible failure is that the policy repeats its previously accepted edit without first querying compatible state evidence. The runtime’s conditional-update guard then blocks the stale write, and the trial exhausts its execution allowance without completing the repair. The training corpus never included that state, so nothing in it taught a correction. While serialization and loss masking preserve the demonstrated decision problem, production deployment dictates which states the agent actually encounters. A policy trained strictly under teacher forcing never observes its own compounding errors during optimization. In standard text generation, an unexpected token merely drifts stylistic phrasing. In an autonomous agent, an errant action mutates the external environment and pushes the agent into states that the demonstration corpus never covered.

Definition 15.3: Exposure bias

Exposure bias is the mismatch between training a sequential policy on reference prefixes and running it on prefixes produced by its own earlier outputs (Bengio et al. 2015; Ranzato et al. 2016).

  1. Significance: It converts a per-step error rate into a horizon-dependent cost. Unchecked horizon compounding (Reliability and Cost over a Horizon) already makes the failure probability roughly \(\TurnHorizon\PerStepErrorRate\) for a small per-step rate \(\PerStepErrorRate\). When that rate is measured solely on reference states, the best available bound on excess task cost is \(\TurnHorizon^2\PerStepErrorRate\) (theorem 15.1, stated below), one further factor of \(\TurnHorizon\).
  2. Distinction: It reflects a structural property of the training distribution rather than an isolated mistake. A policy can err on states the corpus covers and recover on states it never showed.
  3. Common pitfall: Interpreting low offline validation loss on recorded prefixes as evidence that deployed execution will succeed. Offline loss samples only the reference trajectory, revealing nothing about the states the deployed policy visits once it deviates.

4 Exposure Bias: Ranzato and colleagues introduced the name in 2016 for text generators trained on reference words and run on their own (Ranzato et al. 2016). Control researchers had met the same effect earlier as the compounding error of behavioral cloning, which Ross and Bagnell bounded in 2010 (Ross and Bagnell 2010). The two literatures describe one mechanism, and agents inherit the control version: a wrong action changes the environment, not just the phrasing.

Ranzato, Marc’Aurelio, Sumit Chopra, Michael Auli, and Wojciech Zaremba. 2016. “Sequence Level Training with Recurrent Neural Networks.” 4th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Ross, Stéphane, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2010. “Efficient Reductions for Imitation Learning.” Proceedings of the Thirteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 9: 661–68.

Offline training corpora can include recovery trajectories, allowing an agent that departs from an ideal demonstration to re-enter a familiar state or discover an alternate valid solution. Fundamentally, exposure bias4 concerns how thoroughly the corpus covers the state distribution generated by the deployed policy itself, including unexpected edge states like the version conflict.

15.6.1 State distributions and imitation regret

An idealized finite-horizon model makes this coverage problem mathematically precise. The notation follows the imitation-learning literature and applies locally to this derivation. Here, the index \(\TurnIndex\) counts discrete decision steps rather than individual token positions. Three symbols are local to this derivation. The state \(\DecisionProcessState\) is the complete environment state of the decision process, including interaction history and system variables the agent cannot directly observe; the \(\TrajectoryState_{t}\) of definition 15.1 named only what the policy conditions on, its token context. The learned policy \(\pi\) is the trained policy \(\Policy\), viewed as a map from states to actions. The reference policy \(\pi^*\) is a deterministic expert whose actions define correct behavior, the demonstrator the corpus imitates. Let \(\TurnHorizon\) denote the horizon length in decision steps, and let both policies operate under identical initial-state distributions and transition dynamics. Let \(\StateDistribution^t\) denote the distribution of states encountered before step \(\TurnIndex\) under policy \(\pi\). The horizon-averaged state distribution is then \(\StateDistribution = \TurnHorizon^{-1}\sum_{t=1}^\TurnHorizon \StateDistribution^t\).

Let \(\ImmediateTaskCostScore(\DecisionProcessState,\PolicyAction)\in[0,1]\) be a normalized immediate task cost and \(\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi)\) its expected sum over the horizon. Let \(\LossFunction(\DecisionProcessState,\pi)=\Pr_{\PolicyAction\sim\pi(\cdot\mid \DecisionProcessState)}[\PolicyAction\ne\pi^*(\DecisionProcessState)]\) measure disagreement with the reference. Disagreement serves as a conservative proxy for task failure, since multiple distinct actions may be acceptable in practice. Furthermore, it quantifies the probability of selecting an off-reference action at decision time, a metric fundamentally distinct from the token-level cross-entropy loss reported during training. Finally, the cost-to-go \(\ReferenceCostToGo(\DecisionProcessState,\PolicyAction)\) is the expected cost from step \(\TurnIndex\) through \(\TurnHorizon\) after taking \(\PolicyAction\) in \(\DecisionProcessState\) and following the reference thereafter.

Theorem 15.1: Imitation regret bounds
The horizon penalty of imitation depends on which states the error is measured on. For a policy \(\pi\) imitating a deterministic reference \(\pi^*\) over \(\TurnHorizon\) steps with per-step cost in \([0,1]\), Ross et al. (2011) bound the excess task cost in two ways.

Reference-state error (their Theorem 2.1, first proved by Ross and Bagnell (2010)). With disagreement measured on the reference policy’s states, \(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate=\mathbb E_{\DecisionProcessState\sim \ReferenceStateDistribution}[\LossFunction(\DecisionProcessState,\pi)]\),

\[\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi)-\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*)\le \min\{\TurnHorizon,\;\TurnHorizon^2\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\}. \tag{5}\]

Learner-state error (their Theorem 2.2). With disagreement measured on the learner’s states, \(\LearnerStateDisagreementRate=\mathbb E_{\DecisionProcessState\sim \StateDistribution}[\LossFunction(\DecisionProcessState,\pi)]\), and every deviation followed by reference recovery costing at most \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) extra, \(\ReferenceCostToGo(\DecisionProcessState,\PolicyAction)-\ReferenceCostToGo(\DecisionProcessState,\pi^*(\DecisionProcessState))\le \DeviationDisadvantage\) at every learner-reachable state,

\[\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi)-\ExpectedTaskCostSum(\pi^*)\le \min\{\TurnHorizon,\;\DeviationDisadvantage \TurnHorizon\LearnerStateDisagreementRate\}. \tag{6}\]

  • Horizon (\(\TurnHorizon\)): decision steps in the task; because each step costs at most one, \(\TurnHorizon\) also caps both bounds.
  • State distributions (\(\ReferenceStateDistribution\), \(\StateDistribution\)): the environment states the reference or the learner visits, averaged over the horizon.
  • Disagreement rates (\(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\), \(\LearnerStateDisagreementRate\)): probability of an action other than the reference’s, averaged over reference-visited vs. learner-visited states.
  • Deviation disadvantage (\(\DeviationDisadvantage\ge0\)): the largest extra expected cost of one deviation followed by reference recovery, an assumption about the task rather than a measured retry count.

The same error rate therefore carries different horizon penalties. At \(\TurnHorizon=\) 30 and a 2 percent disagreement rate, the reference-state bound is 18 cost units and the learner-state bound with \(\DeviationDisadvantage=\) 1 is 0.6, a gap of 30×, which is \(\TurnHorizon/\DeviationDisadvantage\). The linear bound holds only while \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) stays independent of \(\TurnHorizon\); one action that can spoil the rest of the task makes \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) grow with \(\TurnHorizon\) and restores the quadratic dependence.

The quadratic term has an intuitive physical reading. Couple the environment randomness of the two policies until they first choose different actions. The probability of any such divergence is at most the sum of disagreement probabilities along the reference process, \(\TurnHorizon\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\), and a divergence can corrupt the entire remaining horizon. This coupling argument makes no assumptions regarding the learner’s error rate once it leaves the reference trajectory. Conditional error bounds in trajectory imitation derives the reference-state bound by coupling and the learner-state bound through telescoping policy comparisons.

Unchecked runtime step errors compound as \((1-\PerStepErrorRate)^\TurnHorizon\) (equation 1.1), and for small \(\PerStepErrorRate\) this is close to \(1-\TurnHorizon\PerStepErrorRate\), so the chance of some failure grows roughly as \(\TurnHorizon\PerStepErrorRate\). Theorem 15.1 adds the training-side consequence, since imitation whose error is measured on the reference distribution is bounded only by \(\TurnHorizon^2\PerStepErrorRate\), a second factor of \(\TurnHorizon\). The two rates are analogous rather than identical. The introduction’s \(\PerStepErrorRate\) is a probability of error, while the disagreement rate counts every departure from the reference, harmless ones included, and so upper-bounds that error probability.

Same 2 percent error: 18 cost units on reference states, 0.6 on learner states.

The bounds are stated in normalized cost units. If each step spent off the reference path costs one unit and every other step costs nothing, the reference-state bound of 18 units caps the expected number of wasted turns at 18 of 30, while the learner-state bound of 0.6 caps it below one. Wasted turns reach the familiar evaluation quantities directly, since they raise the expected attempt cost in \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) and, once they exhaust the turn budget, lower the acceptance probability. The deviation disadvantage \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) is an assumption about the task and its reference, not a property of the trained learner; it asks how much extra cost one slip adds when the reference takes over afterward. A repair edit that the runtime refuses before dispatch, or that a version guard blocks, costs a small, fixed number of extra turns to correct (in the recorded trace, one schema inspection and one reissued call) however long the task is, which is why a small \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) that does not grow with \(\TurnHorizon\) is plausible for the repair agent.

Retrospective recovery records help only to the extent that they populate the training corpus with learner-visited states and keep \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) small, but an offline dataset alone cannot guarantee that either assumption holds during deployment.

Napkin Math 15.3: The same error rate under different assumptions
Problem: An illustrative task has horizon 30 and immediate cost bounded between zero and one. Compare a 2 percent disagreement rate on reference-policy states with the same numerical rate on learner-policy states. What changes if the latter also satisfies a deviation-disadvantage bound of \(\DeviationDisadvantage=\) 1, and how accurate would offline imitation have to be to match it?

Math: The reference-state bound multiplies \(\TurnHorizon^2=\) 900 by \(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate=\) 0.02, giving 18 normalized cost units, below the cap of 30. The learner-state bound multiplies \(\DeviationDisadvantage \TurnHorizon=\) 30 by the same rate, giving 0.6. If the disadvantage instead reaches \(\DeviationDisadvantage=\TurnHorizon\), the learner bound returns to 18. Matching the learner-state bound on reference states requires \(\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\) equal to that bound divided by \(\TurnHorizon^2\), about 0.07 percent.

Result: At this horizon the two bounds differ by 30× for the same measured error rate.

Systems insight: The gap is \(\TurnHorizon/\DeviationDisadvantage\), so it grows linearly with the horizon. Offline imitation must be \(\TurnHorizon/\DeviationDisadvantage\) times more accurate on reference states to carry the guarantee that labeling learner-visited states provides. The reference-state bound also saturates at its cap once \(\TurnHorizon\ge 1/\ReferenceStateDisagreementRate\), here 50 steps, beyond which it constrains nothing. Long-horizon agents with cheap, recoverable deviations are therefore where on-policy labeling pays most. When one deviation can spoil the task (\(\DeviationDisadvantage\) near \(\TurnHorizon\)), the learner-state bound is no better than the quadratic one, and the runtime checks that invariant closure (principle 1) requires carry the reliability.

15.6.2 Dataset aggregation

Dataset Aggregation (DAgger) addresses distribution shift systematically: it rolls out trajectories under successive learned policies, queries an authorized reference oracle at the states they visit, aggregates those newly labeled transitions into the training corpus, and retrains the policy5 (Ross et al. 2011). The resulting dataset constitutes on-policy data, drawn directly from the state distribution generated by the policy being trained. Executing a learner action in the environment and generating its reference label are distinct operations, and early collection rounds interleave them. In round \(\LabelingRoundIndex\), the data collection worker executes the reference action with probability \(\ReferenceMixingProbability_k\) and the learner’s predicted action otherwise, decaying \(\ReferenceMixingProbability_k\) toward zero across rounds. This schedule moves the collected states toward the learner’s own distribution, which is the distribution on which the learner-state bound of theorem 15.1 measures error. Aggregation broadens coverage but does not make the training and learner distributions equal, and the linear bound still holds only where the deviation disadvantage \(\DeviationDisadvantage\) stays small, so each retrained round has to be checked on fresh rollouts rather than presumed to enjoy it.

5 DAgger’s Mixing Schedule: Ross, Gordon, and Bagnell require only that the average reference-execution probability tend to zero over rounds, and report that the simplest schedule, executing the reference only in the first round, often performs best. The systems cost sits elsewhere: every visited state needs a reference label on demand, so the labeler is an online service in the collection loop rather than a one-time annotation pass.

An unexpected version conflict exemplifies exactly such an off-distribution state. In automated system repair, the engineering challenge is ensuring that an authorized labeler can supply a valid corrective action once that conflict arises. The labeler, typically a larger foundation model or a search procedure with an expanded inference budget, produces proposals that may themselves contain errors, and a faulty label actively trains recovery in the wrong direction. The curation pipeline therefore executes the proposed corrective step inside an isolated sandbox and retains the label only if automated verification passes under the consumer-specific predicates established in Admission predicates. An accepted continuation confirms that the label functions correctly, not that it represents the sole viable action. Labeling also extends beyond overt runtime rejections: steps that appear superficially successful may still rely on ungrounded assumptions or violated preconditions. The pipeline must therefore record ground-truth observations, enforce tool interface contracts, and track provenance for disputed labels.

Retraining modifies the policy parameters, which shifts the states the policy visits during execution. As a result, the updated policy must be evaluated against fresh bounded rollouts and held-out test suites rather than the accumulated training buffer, which reflects obsolete distributions from earlier iterations.

Fault injection supplies off-path states deliberately rather than waiting for the learner to reach them. In a fault-injected record, the synthesized error is presented as an observation with loss mask \(\PositionLossMask_{t} = 0\), and the diagnosis and corrective tool invocation that follow are targets with \(\PositionLossMask_{t} = 1\), so the gradient lands on decision logic that nominal demonstrations never exercise. The synthetic targets must preserve exact tool contracts and system semantics. A pre-dispatch argument refusal and a lost acknowledgment after a state-mutating call demand different corrections, because the second leaves the effect unknown, and settlement before retry (principle 8) requires resolving it first; a record that teaches a blind retry there teaches the duplicate effect the runtime exists to prevent. Fault injection covers the failures the harness designers anticipated, whereas on-policy collection uncovers the ones the model’s own behavior produces (figure 4). Both are choices of behavior source in the sense of section 15.2.2, and both still need a step-level label for every selected decision. Reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards drops that requirement by scoring fresh rollouts against terminal outcomes instead.

↪ Optimizing a policy against verifiable outcomes on fresh rollouts, without step-level labels, is developed in Foundations of Verifiable Reward Optimization.

Checkpoint 15.2: Coverage after faithful imitation

State coverage encompasses both environment variables and the tool contracts presented at each decision step. An agent may recover reliably from familiar runtime exceptions yet fail persistently by emitting deprecated argument names following an interface update. Schema robustness tests this narrower sensitivity to structured tool definitions.

15.7 Schema Robustness

When a training trajectory captures an agent inspecting a tool schema before repairing a failed invocation, fine-tuning teaches the policy to consult tool definitions before emitting an action. However, consulting a schema and correctly adhering to an altered interface contract represent two distinct capabilities. If a training corpus exposes the model to only a single, static tool signature across thousands of examples, the policy can memorize specific parameter keys and argument types into its weights instead of dynamically reading the contract provided in the prompt context. Evaluating whether a model exhibits genuine schema conditioning requires perturbing the presented interface definition while keeping the underlying task constant.

Consider a read-tool interface update. The active runtime interface requires a string argument named target_uri, strictly forbids extraneous keys, and resolves paths relative to an authorized fixture directory. A policy that memorized an earlier interface iteration instead emits the legacy parameter name file_path:

{
  "proposed_call": {
    "name": "read_file",
    "arguments": {"file_path": "src/pool.py"}
  },
  "current_argument_schema": {
    "type": "object",
    "properties": {"target_uri": {"type": "string"}},
    "required": ["target_uri"],
    "additionalProperties": false
  }
}

This proposal violates the presented schema because it omits the required target_uri property and supplies the disallowed file_path key. Because the agent runtime validates invocations before dispatch, the schema validator rejects the call immediately. The compatible payload is {"target_uri": "src/pool.py"}. Beyond syntactic validity, whether that path exists and whether the agent holds authorization to access it remain independent downstream runtime checks.

Emitting stale parameter keys is a clear, observable failure mode of interface memorization. Attributing this error pattern to trajectory fine-tuning, however, requires careful empirical comparison. Base foundation models also make schema errors when prompted zero-shot. Isolating the effect of fine-tuning demands evaluating both the base and fine-tuned policies under identical prompts, sampling parameters, and tool schemas; section 15.11 formalizes the exact error metrics and comparison procedures required to establish this attribution.

15.7.1 Schema augmentation

Schema augmentation synthesizes training trajectories with declared interface mutations to force the policy to bind its tool calls to the prompt context rather than internal weight priors. A valid schema transformation involves substantially more than performing string substitution on the tool call. Renaming a parameter, shuffling arguments, or modifying declarations reshapes the entire decision context. Every surrounding artifact, including schema definitions, generated calls, mock observations, and error messages, must remain mutually consistent. Because these synthetic trajectories modify real interaction records, their data lineage and validity bounds must be audited under the trajectory provenance framework. Conditioning policies directly on delivered interface contracts is well established: for example, incorporating retrieved API documentation into training prompts allows fine-tuned models to adapt smoothly when documentation changes after deployment (Patil et al. 2023).

Patil, Shishir G., Tianjun Zhang, Xin Wang, and Joseph E. Gonzalez. 2023. “Gorilla: Large Language Model Connected with Massive APIs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2305.15334, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2305.15334.

↩︎ The rule that a transformed trajectory becomes a new derived version bound to its parent trace is established in Derived records.

Table 9 distinguishes useful transformations from their consistency obligations. Each operator breaks a specific shortcut the policy might otherwise exploit, such as relying on a fixed field order or hardcoded identifier names.

In the read-tool example, implementing a semantic-preserving rename requires an adapter that maps the new identifier target_uri to the underlying filesystem operation. Furthermore, any prior tool outputs, system prompts, or conversational turns that reference the historical file_path identifier must be updated across the trajectory context. The resulting record must illustrate a coherent interaction under the modified interface without falsely claiming that the altered bytes occurred during the original historical run.
