If a transformation alters return values, ordering, default configurations, or operational side effects, systems engineers must generate valid execution evidence, either by replaying the trajectory against an instrumented sandbox or synthesizing verified responses, rather than splicing stale historical observations onto modified calls.

These transformations also introduce measurable engineering and computational costs. Renaming identifiers can alter subword tokenization boundaries and token counts. Adding distractor tools expands the prompt context for every step in the trajectory, directly increasing training FLOPs and inflating key-value cache memory footprints during inference. Every synthetic example also expands the corpus that verification pipelines must inspect. Systems teams should therefore version each schema transformation pipeline, track token expansion ratios, and quantify whether the resulting robustness gains justify the additional training and review overhead.

15.7.2 Transfer evaluation

Evaluation suites must explicitly distinguish among three distinct operational regimes: novel tasks executed against familiar tool schemas, familiar tasks executed against renamed or restructured interfaces, and novel tasks targeting entirely unfamiliar tool families. Conflating these regimes obscures the specific failure modes of a policy. Evaluation splits must protect task answers and synthetic variants. Reusing familiar tool schemas across train and test partitions does not constitute data contamination when the experimental objective specifically evaluates task-level transfer under a known interface.

↩︎ The isolation of held-out task answers, and the rule that transformed records inherit their parent’s split group, are established in Held-out evidence protection.

Evaluation pipelines should decouple three hierarchical criteria: structural schema conformance, contextual tool selection, and overall task completion. A proposed invocation can adhere to the JSON Schema validator perfectly yet select the wrong tool, target an unauthorized resource, or supply arguments that fail to solve the user’s objective. Measuring each dimension independently prevents engineers from mistaking syntactic compliance for operational success. When assessing schema augmentation, compare fine-tuned policies against unaugmented baselines under an equivalent token budget, measuring performance on both familiar in-distribution schemas and perturbed transfer interfaces to ensure the model retains core capabilities while gaining interface robustness.

Schema augmentation remains a dataset engineering technique governed by empirical validation, and the runtime proposal boundary must continue to validate every generated call against the active interface contract before execution. Supervised imitation fine-tuning teaches an agent to produce valid tool proposals that match demonstration distributions. When multiple distinct tool proposals satisfy the schema but differ in latency, monetary cost, or operational risk, the training objective must move beyond pure imitation to optimize justified preferences among competing, valid actions.

15.8 Trajectory Preference Optimization

Consider two independently collected continuations of the connection-pool repair that start from identical diagnostic context and both successfully resolve the leak. One continuation runs the entire integration test suite after every minor edit, while the other runs targeted unit tests and executes the full suite only upon completion. Supervised imitation of both trajectories teaches the agent wasteful verification habits alongside disciplined ones; conversely, training solely on the efficient path discards the valuable signal that the slower trajectory was functionally correct yet computationally expensive. A preference comparison captures this relative trade-off directly, steering the policy toward the efficient trajectory without penalizing the slower candidate as a failed repair. However, not every pair of recorded interactions constitutes an admissible preference comparison. In the recovery demonstration, for instance, the malformed proposal e2 and the subsequent correction e4 cannot be paired as preferences, because the correction conditioned on additional environmental error messages that e2 never observed. Valid comparisons require compatible alternative decisions evaluated under identical available information.

↩︎ The preference-pair admission predicates, which require competing trajectories to branch from identical conditioning contexts, are established in Admission predicates.

15.8.1 The trajectory preference loss

Let \(\ComparisonContext\) identify the shared comparison context and evaluation criterion, and let \(\PreferredTrajectory\) and \(\DispreferredTrajectory\) denote the preferred and dispreferred trajectory records, respectively. These subscripts reflect the relative ranking assigned during data curation rather than absolute binary correctness. The parameterized policy being trained is \(\Policy\), while \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) denotes a frozen reference policy (typically the supervised fine-tuned checkpoint) against which likelihood shifts are measured and regularized. The word “reference” now names a different role than it did for \(\pi^*\) in section 15.6. There, the reference was the expert whose actions defined correct behavior; here, \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) is only the starting checkpoint the update is measured against, and it is not assumed to be correct. Both policies must assign nonzero probability to all scored tokens under the specified input prefixes.

For each trajectory, let \(\InteractionHistory_t\) denote the rendered context prefix used to predict action token \(\SequenceToken_t\), and let \(\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)\) denote the set of policy-generated token indices selected by the preference objective. Define the reference-relative score as

\[\ReferenceRelativeScore(\Trajectory;\ComparisonContext)=\sum_{t\in\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)} \log\frac{\Policy(\SequenceToken_t\mid \InteractionHistory_t)}{\pi_{\text{ref}}(\SequenceToken_t\mid \InteractionHistory_t)}.\]

Environment observations interleaved within \(\InteractionHistory_t\) condition subsequent predictions but carry no policy likelihood terms. The token selection set \(\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)\) is customized specifically for each comparison. The dispreferred trajectory is evaluated solely to construct a negative contrast; it never serves as an imitation target. Reusing a supervised fine-tuning mask unchanged would discard the unselected, sub-optimal actions that preference optimization is explicitly designed to suppress.

Earlier reinforcement learning from human feedback pipelines fit a separate reward model to pairwise comparisons and then optimized the policy against that learned surrogate using reinforcement learning (Christiano et al. 2017; Ouyang et al. 2022). Direct preference optimization (DPO) bypasses the reward model entirely by optimizing a logistic loss directly over the difference of policy-to-reference log-probability ratios (Rafailov et al. 2023). Using the standard sigmoid function \(\SigmoidFunction(\PreferenceMargin)=1/(1+e^{-\PreferenceMargin})\), a positive preference scale \(\PreferenceScale\) that sets how strongly the objective resists drift from \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\), and a dataset \(\PreferencePairDataset\) of curated comparison pairs, the resulting objective over selected tokens is

\[\DpoLoss=-\mathbb E_{(\ComparisonContext,\PreferredTrajectory,\DispreferredTrajectory)\sim\PreferencePairDataset} \log\SigmoidFunction\!\left(\PreferenceScale[\ReferenceRelativeScore(\PreferredTrajectory;\ComparisonContext)-\ReferenceRelativeScore(\DispreferredTrajectory;\ComparisonContext)]\right). \tag{7}\]

The classic DPO derivation applies to single-turn responses generated for an identical prompt under the Bradley–Terry preference model6 (Bradley and Terry 1952) and penalizes drift from \(\pi_{\text{ref}}\) with a Kullback–Leibler divergence, the expected log-ratio of two distributions’ probabilities, which is zero only when they match. The penalty is taken in the “reverse” direction, with the expectation over the trained policy’s own outputs rather than the reference’s. In outline, the best policy under that penalty is the reference reweighted by the exponentiated reward and divided by a per-context normalizing term (the partition function) that makes its probabilities sum to one. Solving that relation for the reward expresses it as \(\PreferenceScale\) times the policy-to-reference log ratio plus \(\PreferenceScale\) times the logarithm of the normalizer. Because both members of a pair share the same context, they share the same normalizer, and it drops out when one score is subtracted from the other, which is all the Bradley–Terry model ever uses. Consequently, the implicit reward is identified only up to an additive constant per prompt context. Applying this framework to multi-step agent trajectories by scoring selected token spans \(\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)\) is a practical engineering surrogate built upon that foundation and validated by rigorous closed-loop evaluation.

6 Bradley–Terry Model: Bradley and Terry’s 1952 model of paired comparisons gives the probability that item \(\FirstComparedItem\) is preferred to item \(\SecondComparedItem\) as a logistic function of the difference of their latent scores. Only score differences are identified, which is why DPO’s implied reward carries an input-dependent constant that cancels within a pair, and why a DPO score has no meaning outside the comparison that produced it.

↩︎ The Kullback–Leibler divergence, used there to compare a baseline’s and a candidate’s tool-call distributions, is established in Offline comparison and shadow evidence.

When an objective scores every policy-generated token across a complete interaction, stationary environment dynamics cancel out in the policy-to-reference ratio for that specific recorded trajectory, because both models evaluate the actions against the identical sequence of historical observations. Between two alternative trajectories, however, the observed states diverge. Those divergent observations alter the conditioning context \(\InteractionHistory_t\) for all downstream actions, ensuring that environmental feedback continues to shape the cumulative score even though the policy assigns no explicit probability to environment outputs.

For a single comparison pair, define the preference margin \(\PreferenceMargin=\PreferenceScale(\PreferredReferenceRelativeScore-\DispreferredReferenceRelativeScore)\) (a new use of \(\PreferenceMargin\), unrelated to the rationale tokens of section 15.4.4), where \(\PreferredReferenceRelativeScore\) and \(\DispreferredReferenceRelativeScore\) represent the reference-relative log-likelihood sums for the preferred and dispreferred trajectories. Differentiating the loss in equation 7 with respect to the policy parameters \(\ModelParameters\) while holding the reference policy fixed yields

\[\nabla_\ModelParameters\DpoLoss=-\PreferenceScale\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin) \left(\sum_{t\in\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\PreferredTrajectory)}\nabla_\ModelParameters\log\Policy(\SequenceToken_t\mid \InteractionHistory_t) -\sum_{t\in\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\DispreferredTrajectory)}\nabla_\ModelParameters\log\Policy(\SequenceToken_t\mid \InteractionHistory_t)\right). \tag{8}\]

This gradient reveals three critical systems dynamics. First, identical selected tokens sharing an identical conditioning prefix contribute equal gradient vectors to both sums in equation 8 and cancel exactly. A shared prefix therefore suffers no negative gradient updates simply because it also appears at the start of the dispreferred record. Once the paths diverge, identical tokens no longer cancel because their conditioning histories differ. Second, because shared model parameters parameterize all tokens, updating weights on selected positions inevitably shifts likelihoods on unselected positions. Third, the full scalar coefficient multiplying the difference of gradient sums is \(\PreferenceScale\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)\); \(\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)=1-\SigmoidFunction(\PreferenceMargin)\) is its saturation factor. At the reference policy, both scores are zero (\(\PreferenceMargin=0\)), so the factor is \(0.5\) and the coefficient is \(\PreferenceScale/2\). At fixed \(\PreferenceScale\), increasing a positive margin reduces both quantities, dampening the contribution of pairs the policy already distinguishes. The coefficient alone does not determine the full gradient norm or the optimizer step, which also depend on the gradient vectors and optimization state.

Napkin Math 15.4: How hard one comparison pulls
Problem: After some training, the policy has moved relative to the reference on one admitted comparison. What are the pair’s margin, loss, saturation factor, and full gradient coefficient, and how do they change if \(\PreferenceScale\) doubles?

Variables: The selected-token log-probability sums are -8 for the active policy and -10 for the reference on the preferred record; the dispreferred record’s sums are -11 and -10. Use \(\PreferenceScale=\) 0.2. These are hypothetical, dimensionless natural-log quantities.

Math: The reference-relative scores are 2 and -1, giving margin \(\PreferenceMargin=\) 0.6, logistic score \(\SigmoidFunction(\PreferenceMargin)=\) 0.646, and pair loss 0.437, down from 0.693 at \(\PreferenceMargin=0\). The saturation factor \(\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)\) falls from 0.5 to 0.354, while the full coefficient \(\PreferenceScale\SigmoidFunction(-\PreferenceMargin)\) falls from 0.1 to 0.0709. At \(\PreferenceScale=\) 0.4, the same log-probabilities give \(\PreferenceMargin=\) 1.2, a logistic score of 0.769, a saturation factor of 0.231, and a full coefficient of 0.0926.

Systems insight: Changing \(\PreferenceScale\) changes both the margin and the gradient prefactor. In this fixed-record comparison, doubling it reduces the saturation factor from 0.354 to 0.231 but increases the full coefficient from 0.0709 to 0.0926. This local coefficient comparison establishes neither the eventual policy’s divergence from the reference nor the size of its optimizer step. The logistic score \(\SigmoidFunction(\PreferenceMargin)\) rises with \(\PreferenceScale\) for the same log-probabilities, reflecting the model’s fit to this comparison rather than the operational success probability of the repair. Furthermore, the margin can increase either by boosting the likelihood of the preferred trajectory or by suppressing the dispreferred trajectory; only closed-loop execution can verify whether the resulting policy generalizes or merely avoids the dispreferred path.

Evaluating an entire trajectory distributes credit and blame across all divergent tokens in the interaction. Conversely, constructing narrower comparisons centered directly at the branch point where alternative tool calls or edits diverge localizes the gradient update to the specific decision that matters. Trajectory length affects the loss through the accumulation of scored tokens: because log-probability ratios can be positive or negative, longer sequences do not introduce a systematic directional bias to the margin, but they do increase score variance. Systems engineers frequently evaluate two objective modifications to mitigate length sensitivity: length-normalizing each score by its selected-token count \(|\PreferenceScoredPositionSet(\Trajectory)|\), and adding an auxiliary supervised imitation loss on the preferred record \(\PreferredTrajectory\) to prevent the model from drifting into low-likelihood degeneration. Both variants represent empirical design choices that must be validated against closed-loop execution benchmarks.

15.8.2 Reference log-probability caching

When training on a static dataset with a frozen reference model, systems pipelines can precompute and cache the reference policy’s log probabilities for all chosen action tokens. When token masks and aggregation rules are fixed, storing only the scalar sum \(\ReferenceLogProbabilitySum(\Trajectory)\) avoids the overhead of caching intermediate token-level values, and neither approach requires materializing full-vocabulary logits. To prevent silent cache staleness across training experiments, the cache key must deterministically bind the reference model weights, any adapter configuration (section 15.10), tokenizer version, chat template formatting, exact tokenized sequences, attention mask structure, position encodings, truncation thresholds, selected token indices, and floating-point precision modes (such as FP16 vs. BF16). Any modification to data preprocessing or token selection invalidates cached sums even when model weights remain unchanged. Systems pipelines should validate cached values against live recomputations on sampled batches within a declared numerical tolerance (for example, \(10^{-5}\)) rather than assuming bitwise identity across accelerator architectures or kernel runtimes.

Caching eliminates the reference model weights and its redundant forward passes from the distributed training loop, trading preprocessing time, disk storage, and host-to-device transfer bandwidth for accelerator memory. The memory saved equals the reference model’s parameter and buffer footprint. However, the active policy’s parameters, optimizer state (the running statistics an adaptive optimizer keeps for each trained parameter), gradient buffers, and forward activations remain in accelerator memory. Quantifying the actual training throughput and memory savings therefore requires a comprehensive memory inventory, built from the component breakdown in section 15.10, rather than assuming a flat percentage reduction.

Trajectory preference optimization modifies the loss function applied to a static corpus of curated alternatives. While active exploration under outcome verifiers is the domain of reinforcement learning, an offline adaptation pipeline must also determine where its demonstration trajectories originate. Labeling rounds and fault injection already answered that question for off-path states. When deploying extensive prompt guidance becomes too costly at inference time, policy distillation transfers those capabilities directly into student weights (section 15.9).

15.9 Policy Distillation for Autonomous Trajectories

Consider an automated repair agent whose system prompt carries a comprehensive debugging checklist and worked examples demonstrating how to inspect a stack trace before revising a tool call. In production, an autonomous agent repeats this entire prompt prefix across every turn of every trajectory. This repeated overhead consumes valuable context-window capacity and inflates time-to-first-token latency, even though the procedural workflow it prescribes belongs in the model weights according to the division of responsibilities in table 1. Training the model to internalize the procedure allows the runtime to drop the checklist from the prompt, reclaiming those tokens on every interaction. Policy distillation transfers behavioral capabilities from an expensive teacher configuration into an efficient student model to reduce deployment costs. The teacher configuration can vary along multiple axes: it may use a larger model family, an expanded prompt with extensive in-context guidance, or a broader test-time search budget. Regardless of the teacher configuration, its emitted trajectories must pass the same target admission criteria as any human or empirical candidate before entering the training pipeline.

15.9.1 Context distillation

Context distillation7 formalizes this transition for prompt-based guidance. The pipeline first samples candidate demonstrations using an expanded prompt that includes the full checklist, diagnostic routines, or few-shot exemplars. After admission filters validate the resulting trajectories, the training pipeline strips the instructional guidance from the prompt and fine-tunes the model to generate the exact same target actions from the abbreviated prefix. Because this transformation deliberately restructures the learning problem rather than reproducing the teacher’s original input, data provenance systems must preserve both prompt variants and the explicit transformation rules within the trajectory’s lineage record (section 15.3).

7 Context Distillation: Askell and colleagues introduced the term in 2021 for training a model without a long prompt to match the output distribution the same model produced with it, motivated by context-window limits and the compute required to process prompt prefixes repeatedly (Askell et al. 2021). The savings recur across every turn of every deployed trajectory, which makes the technique especially valuable for operational instructions that are extensive, stable, and repeatedly sent across the session.

Askell, Amanda, Yuntao Bai, Anna Chen, et al. 2021. “A General Language Assistant as a Laboratory for Alignment.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2112.00861, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2112.00861.

Pruning static system guidance differs fundamentally from stripping dynamic environmental feedback, such as an authorization refusal or a schema validation error that justified a corrective tool call. Removing causal observations leaves the corrected action unsupported by its surrounding context, effectively training the student to execute state changes without diagnostic justification. When transforming a recorded execution such as trace_r19_01_redacted_v1, the distillation pipeline produces a distinct, versioned artifact with its own target review; the historical runtime outcome remains bound to the original long-prompt execution.

The architectural boundary established in table 1 governs what can be distilled into weights and what must remain in the prompt. Procedural heuristics can migrate into model parameters, whereas operational contracts cannot. Current task facts, external tool schemas, and authorization policies must remain in the runtime context. If a distillation pipeline bakes API schemas directly into the weights alongside procedural checklists, the resulting model becomes brittle and fails the moment an external service updates its argument structure.

This boundary also dictates when to trigger retraining. Modifying a procedural heuristic, such as a new recovery policy for failed HTTP requests, requires curating new data, launching a fine-tuning run, and passing automated release gates. By contrast, changing an operational contract requires only an update to the system prompt or tool configuration. When operational guidance evolves faster than the pipeline can train, evaluate, and deploy a new checkpoint, that guidance functions as a contract and must stay in-context. A business rule that changes on a weekly cadence, for example, is an operational contract in practice.

Validation must evaluate the student policy using the actual abbreviated production prompt. Teams must verify whether the targeted procedure successfully transferred, whether task completion and tool-calling accuracy held steady, and whether downstream token reductions materialized. Engineers should avoid estimating latency improvements purely from arithmetic reductions in prompt tokens. Real-world end-to-end latency depends on KV-cache reuse, output generation length, tool execution overhead, and the frequency of recovery loops. A distilled agent that processes fewer input tokens but initiates an extra round-trip tool call to recover from a mistyped argument will easily wipe out any prefill latency savings.

15.9.2 Teacher distillation

In classical machine learning, knowledge distillation compresses an ensemble or large teacher model into a compact student by training on the teacher’s full probability distribution8 (Buciluă et al. 2006; Hinton et al. 2015). In agent systems, however, trajectory collectors rarely have access to the teacher’s internal logits. When querying proprietary frontier models or remote service endpoints, the collector observes only discrete sampled tokens: generated tool calls, structured arguments, and conversational steps. Trajectory distillation therefore operates as imitation learning on discrete sampled trajectories rather than distribution matching.

8 Soft Targets: Buciluǎ, Caruana, and Niculescu-Mizil compressed large ensembles in 2006 by labeling data with the ensemble’s predictions and training a small network on those labels; Hinton, Vinyals, and Dean in 2015 named the method distillation and trained on the teacher’s temperature-softened probabilities over every class. Soft targets require the teacher’s logits. An agent collector that sees only a teacher’s sampled messages has one sampled token per position instead of a distribution, so trajectory distillation is imitation of sampled responses and needs the same target admission as any other demonstration.

The teacher configuration may consist of a larger parameter-scale model, an inference pipeline equipped with an expanded search budget (such as Monte Carlo tree search or best-of-\(\CandidatePoolSize\) sampling), or the same base model running with extensive in-context guidance. Regardless of the teacher’s sophistication, its candidate trajectories must pass consumer-specific admission filters. A teacher’s fluency, reasoning length, or self-reported confidence does not constitute an objective correctness check. Fine-tuning compact models on agent trajectories generated by stronger teacher models consistently improves tool-use proficiency and execution stability compared to prompting the student in zero-shot configurations (Chen et al. 2023; Zeng et al. 2023).

This training paradigm does not always require an external teacher. In self-taught reasoning architectures such as STaR (Zelikman et al. 2022), the model samples multiple trajectory candidates per task, automated verifiers isolate the subset of successful rollouts, and the policy trains on its own verified successes. However, relying on self-generated rollouts introduces the compounding selection and feedback risks analyzed in The Self-Improvement Loop.

Zelikman, Eric, Yuhuai Wu, Jesse Mu, and Noah D. Goodman. 2022. “STaR: Bootstrapping Reasoning with Reasoning.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 15476–88.

A student model can generalize differently from the teacher. Because the data curation pipeline admits only verified successes, the student trains on a filtered distribution that is systematically cleaner than the teacher’s raw output stream. Consequently, no theoretical ceiling limits the student’s task completion rate to that of the unguided teacher. Conversely, the student also internalizes any systematic vulnerabilities, inefficient tool patterns, or subtle biases in the teacher that slip past automated target review.

Distillation governs how training trajectories are sourced, conditioned, and curated, independently of the loss formulation and the parameter artifact distinguished in section 15.2.2. Whether a team then updates every weight or a small adapter, the resulting policy is bounded by the quality and causal integrity of its admitted demonstrations. What the artifact choice does change is the cost of holding the update, and that cost multiplies once a deployment trains a separate student for each agent role.

15.10 Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning

Full fine-tuning of an agent policy updates and stores a complete replica of every base parameter, requiring the training system to allocate memory for base weights, gradients, and optimizer state across the entire model. For an adaptive-moment optimizer under mixed precision, that state is a higher-precision master copy of each weight plus two running moments, so each trained parameter carries several times the bytes of its weight. Training a second specialized agent role, such as a diagnostic planner or an execution monitor, duplicates that resource footprint in full. Parameter-efficient fine-tuning avoids this multiplicative overhead by freezing the base model and training only a compact set of auxiliary parameters known as an adapter9 (Houlsby et al. 2019), which is distributed alongside the immutable base checkpoint. For systems operating multiple specialized agent roles, lightweight adapters streamline storage and version management. Adapters give modularity, not isolation. An adapter is only a set of weight matrices; like the rest of the weights, it shapes proposals and holds no permissions of its own. Roles whose adapters are served from one process on one shared base, however, share that process’s failure domain and resource limits, so a faulty or corrupted adapter can exhaust memory or stall requests for every role it shares the process with. Separating what roles may do still requires separate runtime grants per role, which no choice of adapter provides.

9 Adapter Modules: Houlsby and colleagues introduced adapters in 2019 as small bottleneck layers inserted inside each Transformer block, updating only those bottleneck parameters per task. However, inserting sequential layers introduces serial computational dependencies during inference. Low-rank adaptation avoids this serving latency by parameterizing updates to match the exact dimensions of existing weight matrices, enabling operators to fold the adapter directly into the base weights when serving without adding serial layer overhead.

Low-rank adaptation (LoRA) expresses the weight update to a selected projection matrix through two low-rank factor matrices (Hu et al. 2021). Let \(\WeightMatrix_0\in\mathbb{R}^{\LayerOutputWidth\times \LayerInputWidth}\) denote a frozen base weight matrix acting on column-vector inputs. LoRA models the adapted matrix as

\[\WeightMatrix=\WeightMatrix_0+\frac{\AdapterScalingFactor}{\AdapterRank}\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix,\qquad \AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\in\mathbb{R}^{\LayerOutputWidth\times \AdapterRank},\quad \AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\in\mathbb{R}^{\AdapterRank\times \LayerInputWidth}.\]

Here, the rank hyperparameter \(\AdapterRank \ll \min(\LayerInputWidth, \LayerOutputWidth)\) bounds the rank of the update, and the scaling factor \(\AdapterScalingFactor/\AdapterRank\) stabilizes optimization across different choices of \(\AdapterRank\). The two factor matrices contain \(\AdapterRank(\LayerInputWidth+\LayerOutputWidth)\) trainable parameters. When sizing a run, engineers must sum this parameter count over all targeted matrices; attention projections and multi-layer perceptron projections frequently differ in dimensionality and need not be square. Target matrix selection and rank operate as primary capacity knobs, tuned directly against empirical validation performance.10

10 Low Intrinsic Rank: Hu and colleagues motivated LoRA by hypothesizing that weight updates during downstream adaptation lie on a manifold with low intrinsic dimension, showing that small ranks (\(\AdapterRank \in \{4, 8\}\)) matched full fine-tuning on standard language benchmarks (Hu et al. 2021). That empirical finding reflects the characteristics of those benchmark tasks rather than a universal theoretical guarantee. Fine-tuning an agent policy on novel tool invocation protocols or structured error recovery often demands specialized capacity, making systematic rank sweeps essential under the evaluation contract of this chapter.

Hu, Edward J., Yelong Shen, Phillip Wallis, Zeyuan Allen-Zhu, Yuanzhi Li, Shean Wang, Lu Wang, and Weizhu Chen. 2021. “LoRA: Low-Rank Adaptation of Large Language Models.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2106.09685.

Initializing \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) from a Gaussian distribution and setting \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix=0\) ensures that \(\FiniteChange\WeightMatrix = \frac{\AdapterScalingFactor}{\AdapterRank}\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) is initially zero, allowing training to begin from the exact forward mapping of the pretrained base model. On the initial backward step, the loss gradient backpropagates directly into \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\), whereas the initial gradient with respect to \(\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\) evaluates to zero because it is scaled by \(\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix=0\). Freezing \(\WeightMatrix_0\) eliminates the memory required for base gradients and optimizer state, yet the backward pass still evaluates matrix-vector products through \(\WeightMatrix_0^T\) to propagate activation gradients down to earlier transformer layers. The resulting gradient with respect to the layer input combines contributions from both the frozen base path and the adapter bypass, as given in LoRA matrix decomposition.

15.10.1 Parameter-state inventory

Training 0.10 percent of the parameters still keeps 12.6 percent of the state.

Systems engineers must size training hardware by auditing memory consumption across specific data structures and numerical representations, rather than relying on nominal parameter counts. Notebook 15.5 compares the resident parameter state of a full fine-tuning run against a LoRA run under a standard mixed-precision storage convention. This inventory focuses strictly on persistent model state; dynamic activation tensors and ephemeral kernel workspaces are analyzed separately, as their footprints fluctuate with context length, batch configuration, and operator implementations.

Napkin Math 15.5: What adapter training removes from the inventory
Problem: A hypothetical model contains 8,000,000,000 base parameters. The run attaches rank-16 adapters to 2 matrices in each of 32 layers. Each selected matrix maps an input width of 4,096 to an output width of 4,096. No other parameters are trained in the adapter case. What parameter-state memory does this save compared with training every base parameter?

For each trainable parameter, assume a weight using 2 bytes, a gradient using 2 bytes, a separate higher-precision master weight that the optimizer updates, using 4 bytes, and two optimizer moments (running averages of the gradient and its square) each using 4 bytes. Frozen parameters retain only their 2 bytes weight copy. This is the mixed-precision accounting of adaptive-moment optimization (Kingma and Ba 2015; Micikevicius et al. 2018), totaling 16 bytes per trainable parameter as modeled by Rajbhandari et al. (2020); it is one declared implementation convention, not a requirement of every optimizer or framework. Count one unsharded resident copy of each array; display capacities in decimal MB and GB.

Math: Each adapted matrix adds \(\AdapterRank(\LayerInputWidth+\LayerOutputWidth)\) parameters, so the adapters contain 32 \(\times\) 2 \(\times\) 16 \(\times\) (4,096 \(+\) 4,096) \(=\) 8,388,608 parameters, 0.10 percent of the base. The convention assigns 16 bytes of state to each trainable parameter and 2 bytes to each frozen one. Table 10 inventories the resulting arrays.

Result: The adapter arrays total 134.22 MB. Including the frozen base gives approximately 16.13 GB, compared with 128 GB for full-parameter training under the same convention, a reduction of approximately 111.87 GB, or 7.9×. The saving comes from dropping gradients, master weights, and optimizer moments for the frozen base.

Systems insight: Even though adapter training updates only 0.10 percent of the parameters, the system must still allocate 12.6 percent of the full-parameter memory state. The frozen base weights remain resident throughout execution and constitute 99.2 percent of what remains. Consequently, total parameter-state savings are fundamentally bounded by the ratio of trainable to frozen bytes per parameter (here, 16 bytes vs. 2 bytes, an 8-fold ceiling). Rank adjustments exert virtually no influence on this footprint; doubling the rank increases memory by only 134.22 MB. The primary engineering lever for shrinking adapter memory state is therefore the numerical precision of the frozen base itself. Dynamic activations and execution workspaces, required under both training paradigms, must be provisioned on top of this subtotal.

Kingma, Diederik P., and Jimmy Ba. 2015. “Adam: A Method for Stochastic Optimization.” 3rd International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

Beyond persistent parameter state, a complete memory plan must budget for forward activations (whether retained in high-bandwidth memory or selectively recomputed), temporary kernel scratchpads, collective communication buffers, allocator fragmentation, and operating headroom. Sharding and offloading distribute these resident arrays across cluster nodes or host memory tiers, trading network or PCIe interconnect bandwidth for device memory headroom. When on-device capacity is severely constrained, quantizing the frozen base weights directly targets the dominant state component, as demonstrated by QLoRA (quantized LoRA) (Dettmers et al. 2023), trading runtime dequantization arithmetic and metadata storage for lower memory occupancy. Because dynamic activation footprints and kernel workspaces scale with context length, batch structure, and attention kernels, engineers should profile these dynamic terms directly on the target hardware cluster before finalizing resource sizing.

15.10.2 Adapter release and compatibility

Releasing an adapter for a specialized agent role requires publishing an explicit configuration manifest alongside the low-rank weights. At minimum, this manifest must record the parent base model version, target projection names, adapter rank \(\AdapterRank\), scaling factor \(\AdapterScalingFactor\), tokenizer revision, prompt templates, and numerical formats. It should also establish provenance by linking the training trajectory corpus, objective formulation, and evaluation audit. Because low-rank factors parameterize deltas relative to a specific parameterization, an adapter is valid only when mounted onto the precise base model version for which it was trained. In addition, serving runtimes must enforce key-value cache compatibility: any cached prefix states generated under one adapter cannot be reused by an adapter that modifies the layers that produced them.

↩︎ The rule that cached key-value prefix state is reusable only under the same base weights and active adapters is established in Prefix compatibility.

Sheng, Ying, Shiyi Cao, Dacheng Li, Coleman Hooper, Shuo Chen, Daxin Jiang, Christopher Rodriguez, et al. 2024. “S-LoRA: Serving Thousands of Concurrent LoRA Adapters.” Proceedings of the 7th Conference on Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys).
Chen, Liyuan, Zihao Ye, Yonghao Wu, Danyang Zhuo, Luis Ceze, and Arvind Krishnamurthy. 2024. “Punica: Multi-Tenant LoRA Serving.” Proceedings of the 7th Conference on Machine Learning and Systems (MLSys).

Maintaining decoupled adapters enables serving runtimes to multiplex multiple agent roles over a single shared base model in accelerator memory. Multi-adapter inference engines batch concurrent requests across distinct adapters while evaluating shared base projections in unified matrix multiplications (Sheng et al. 2024; Chen et al. 2024). Although this architecture amortizes base weight memory across tenants, each role still incurs storage and memory overhead for its factor matrices and dynamic activations. Sharing one serving process across agent roles, however, provides no fault containment between them. A corrupted adapter or an anomalous input runs in the same process and under the same resource limits as every other role, and if the runtime issues tool grants per process rather than per role, the roles also share one authority. Distinct roles therefore need distinct runtime grants; the adapter boundary is not an authority boundary. Where concurrency demands do not justify multi-tenant adapter scheduling, operators can fold the adapter directly into the base weights (\(\WeightMatrix = \WeightMatrix_0 + \frac{\AdapterScalingFactor}{\AdapterRank}\AdapterUpProjectionMatrix\AdapterDownProjectionMatrix\)). Merging eliminates per-token adapter kernel overhead and simplifies deployment into a standalone dense artifact, sacrificing multi-adapter sharing to gain execution simplicity.

When an adapter update exhibits regressions in production, rolling back the deployment steers incoming task admissions back to a previously verified release configuration. Model-level rollback, however, cannot retract tool side effects already committed to external systems (database updates, network dispatches, or filesystem modifications), nor does it automatically resolve inflight operations. The release policy must therefore specify whether active multi-turn trajectories continue executing under their original configuration or transition to the fallback policy under established recovery rules. Validating whether an adapter’s shifted action distribution genuinely enhances end-to-end task completion without degrading reliability remains the decisive operational gate before promoting any learned policy into production.

15.11 Evaluating a Fine-Tuned Agent

A fine-tuned checkpoint can achieve an impressively low loss on a repair corpus yet fail to resolve actual connection-pool leaks in production. Training configurations and data manifests record how an artifact was produced, but only closed-loop trials reveal how the policy navigates multi-step execution when its own actions dictate future observations. The task acceptance contract defined in The Task Acceptance Contract governs these trials, addressing the specific failure modes and behavioral shifts that weight updates introduce.

15.11.1 Comparable loss and closed-loop performance

Held-out token loss measures prediction accuracy under teacher forcing, where pristine historical prefixes are supplied at every step. This metric helps detect optimization failures and compares raw sequence modeling across checkpoints. Autonomous task completion, however, operates under fundamentally different dynamics: a deployed policy generates its own context history, encounters the cascading consequences of its prior mistakes, and consumes a bounded budget of wall-clock time and tokens. Incorporating recovery trajectories into validation sets helps verify whether a model can recognize and correct known errors, but only closed-loop rollouts expose whether the agent can navigate compounding distribution shift across entire trajectories.

Even offline loss comparisons require an identical measurement contract before they can guide engineering decisions. If two training runs use different target loss masks, their loss values average over fundamentally different token distributions. Changing example weighting or loss normalization similarly shifts their relative contributions. In notebook 15.1, for example, an unmasked causal objective averages loss across 16,383 prediction terms, whereas a selected-token objective isolates the 800 selected response tokens generated by the model. Read-only environment observations separately occupy 77.1 percent of the stored input. Comparing raw training dashboard losses across these runs compares fundamentally different quantities. Valid offline comparisons require recomputing a uniform held-out loss over an identical test split using the exact same prompt contexts, tokenizer, serialization template, target mask, and token-normalization rule. Systems engineers should always log both the active-target denominator and the evaluation corpus version alongside this loss. Furthermore, cross-checkpoint loss comparisons become invalid if tokenizers differ: because different tokenizers partition text into different subword spans, their per-token losses do not share a common physical unit.

Example 15.1: A lower loss is not yet a release decision
Scenario: In an evaluation bake-off, two candidate checkpoints are trained on the same curated trajectories: one with an all-token loss and the other with a selected-target response mask. The run trained on all tokens displays a substantially lower dashboard loss.

Diagnosis: The two training metrics calculate averages over incompatible target sets. The all-token run lowers its average by predicting the repetitive parts of environment observations (echoed schemas, boilerplate, repeated paths), whereas the masked run evaluates only policy tokens. Evaluating both checkpoints on an identical held-out validation set using matched response masks makes their loss values comparable. Even a checkpoint with superior held-out loss, however, is evaluated solely on teacher-forced prefixes; during autonomous rollouts, compounding errors can still cause it to invoke invalid tools, misinterpret unexpected exceptions, or blow past its step budget.

Systems lesson: Rank candidate checkpoints offline using an identical held-out evaluation loss, but base promotion and release decisions exclusively on closed-loop task acceptance. A checkpoint boasting lower loss remains unpromotable if it fails the recovery criteria specified in the release contract. When modifying training data and loss masks simultaneously, run controlled ablation trials to isolate the source of any observed performance shift.

15.11.2 Task and behavior measurements

Candidate models must always be evaluated against the complete existing policy stack they are intended to replace. No single syntax check or loss metric can substitute for closed-loop task acceptance; engineers must examine a scorecard of complementary system behaviors.

Budgeted task acceptance is the primary evaluation outcome. Evaluations must use the declared criteria, permitted runtime feedback, pinned fixture versions, and resource envelopes of the task acceptance contract. Every initiated trial belongs in the accounting ledger; trials that stall or time out under the observation cutoff must be recorded as failures or unresolved verdicts rather than discarded. If the target deployment architecture wraps model generation in a selection or repair loop, evaluate that end-to-end composite policy rather than the raw checkpoint in isolation. Candidate availability, single-attempt pass rates (pass@1), and repeated-run consistency (pass\(^\IndependentAttemptCount\)) provide valuable diagnostic views under the assumptions of Potential and repeated-run consistency, while the selection mechanism chosen for production must be benchmarked on its direct end-to-end performance.

Call validity verifies that model-generated tool invocations conform strictly to the active interface contract. Pass proposed invocations through the production parser and schema validator, validating required fields, enum and scalar type constraints, and rules governing unexpected additional properties. Clearly define the denominator of evaluated tool calls, count every validation failure, and distinguish unparsable outputs from proposals cut short by token limits. Syntactic validity does not imply operational correctness. A schema-valid tool call may still invoke an unhelpful tool, request unauthorized access, or issue destructive commands. Track proposal validity separately from downstream environment admission and task execution results.

Table 11 maps each observed failure category to the evidence that separates its competing causes.

Schema robustness assesses whether an adapted model can generalize to modified tool interfaces without falling back on memorized signatures. In production systems, API signatures frequently evolve. Evaluation suites should therefore measure baseline performance on familiar interfaces separately from transformed schemas or held-out tool families, mirroring the evaluation protocols used for unseen APIs in tool-use benchmarks (Qin et al. 2024). Whenever test fixtures rename parameters, the harness must update mock tool implementations and call-site expectations in lockstep, as outlined in section 15.7. Two formal metrics capture this behavior over \(\ScoredToolCallCount\) proposed calls \(\ScoredToolCall_i\), each evaluated against an active schema set \(\ActiveSchemaSet_i\). Schema-following accuracy is the fraction of calls that name a declared tool and satisfy its structural type schema:

Qin, Yujia, Shihao Liang, Yining Ye, Kunlun Zhu, Lan Yan, Yaxi Lu, Yankai Lin, et al. 2024. “ToolLLM: Facilitating Large Language Models to Master 16000+ Real-World APIs.” Proceedings of the 12th International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).

\[\text{SFA}=\frac{1}{\ScoredToolCallCount}\sum_{i=1}^{\ScoredToolCallCount}\EventIndicator\{\operatorname{tool}(\ScoredToolCall_i)\in\ActiveSchemaSet_i\ \wedge\ \ScoredToolCall_i\models\ActiveSchemaSet_i\}.\]

Parametric recall error is the fraction of calls that emit parameter names found in the training key set \(\TrainingSchemaKeySet\) that the currently provided schema does not contain:

\[\text{PRE}=\frac{1}{\ScoredToolCallCount}\sum_{i=1}^{\ScoredToolCallCount}\EventIndicator\{\exists \ToolArgumentKey\in\operatorname{keys}(\ScoredToolCall_i):\ToolArgumentKey\in\TrainingSchemaKeySet\wedge \ToolArgumentKey\notin\operatorname{params}(\ActiveSchemaSet_i)\}.\]

When an adapted agent shows improving success on familiar schemas while its PRE simultaneously climbs on modified schemas, the pattern suggests reliance on obsolete parameter names rather than the delivered schema. Before concluding that parametric memorization is the culprit, engineers should audit prompt templates and serving configurations to verify that the obsolete parameter name was not accidentally injected upstream.

Resource use quantifies how efficiently a candidate policy expends its computational and economic budget. Evaluation harnesses should track wall-clock latency, trajectory turns, generated token volumes, and API costs as independent variables. The key economic metric is the effective cost per accepted task, denoted \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) in equation 1.2, which aggregates the costs of all initiated attempts (successful, failed, and aborted) divided by the count of accepted solutions within the accounting boundary. Because \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) scales inversely with task acceptance probability, a fine-tuned model that takes longer, more deliberate trajectories can yield a lower \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) than an erratic baseline that fails fast. The total cost calculation must incorporate token spend from rejected calls, failed retries, runtime verification passes, and environment cleanup. When a candidate completes zero tasks during an evaluation run, engineers should report total expenditure alongside the zero-acceptance count rather than presenting an undefined or infinite ratio.

↩︎ Comparing policies under one fixed resource envelope, with time and tokens reported separately from monetary charges, is established in Matched-budget comparison.

11 Catastrophic Forgetting: McCloskey and Cohen showed in 1989 that a connectionist network trained sequentially on a new task can lose an earlier one almost entirely, because both share the same weights. A repair-trained policy shares its weights with every other behavior the agent still needs, so retention is measured on the release’s required task set rather than assumed from a narrow training corpus.

McCloskey, Michael, and Neal J. Cohen. 1989. “Catastrophic Interference in Connectionist Networks: The Sequential Learning Problem.” Psychology of Learning and Motivation 24: 109–65. https://doi.org/10.1016/S0079-7421(08)60536-8.

Capability retention monitors whether specialized fine-tuning has degraded baseline abilities required in production. Rather than relying solely on generic language benchmarks, engineers should assemble a targeted regression suite derived from the agent’s actual operational scope. When evaluation reveals a performance drop on core tasks, engineers must audit both the fine-tuning recipe and the serving infrastructure before concluding that the weights suffered catastrophic forgetting11 (McCloskey and Cohen 1989). If regression is confirmed, each prospective mitigation, such as adjusting data mixture ratios, reducing learning rates, or adopting parameter-efficient adapter architectures, must be validated through the same rigorous testing regimen.

15.11.3 Controlled comparison and conditional release

Reproducible evaluation requires binding every benchmark result to an immutable artifact manifest: the specific checkpoint or adapter weights, the base model hash, tokenizer version, serialization template, decoding parameters, and evaluation harness commit. Fix all environment fixtures, evaluation criteria, resource allowances, and repetition seeds across runs. Even under deterministic low-temperature decoding, agent execution exhibits nondeterminism because external tool latency, asynchronous network interactions, and environment states vary; multi-trial repetition remains mandatory. Hermetic fixture isolation, pinned container images, grader sandboxing, and complete trajectory logging enforce reproducibility guarantees.

Prevent benchmark contamination by enforcing strict data-isolation barriers between training corpora and evaluation splits. Re-prompted attempts, redacted execution traces, and paraphrased problem variants must inherit the contamination boundaries of their source tasks. While benchmarking on familiar repositories or APIs reflects legitimate deployment requirements, demonstrating true generalization demands held-out schema and environment families. Engineers should select checkpoint candidates and tuning mixtures using validation splits alone. If failures observed during final testing are fed back into training or prompt tuning, the test suite loses its holdout validity; teams must log this role transition and generate a fresh, uncontaminated test split.

Isolate individual variables when benchmarking an adaptation strategy. Modifying weights, system prompts, and tool definitions simultaneously evaluates a bundled deployment rather than proving the efficacy of the weight update itself. Controlled ablations must compare the fine-tuned checkpoint against the incumbent policy, as well as against the prompt-engineering, schema-refactoring, and runtime-guardrail alternatives prioritized by the intervention ladder. Because the candidate and the incumbent can run on the same tasks, the comparison is a paired one, and the paired-outcome discipline applies unchanged. Count the discordant tasks, report an interval on the difference rather than two overlapping single-arm intervals, and allocate new tasks and within-task repeats according to their marginal variance reduction and fixture/run costs. Reading several ablations at once adds one correction beyond a single pair. If each comparison is tested at the same significance level, the chance that at least one of them shows a false win grows with their number, so the per-comparison level must be tightened; dividing it by the number of comparisons is the simplest such rule. A release claim resting on one seed or one favorable run is a sample of one.

↩︎ Paired comparison of two configurations on the same tasks, with intervals on the difference and the rule for splitting trials between new tasks and repeats, is established in Statistical Evaluation Rigor.

A production release decision combines verified functional improvement with strict behavioral and budgetary guarantees. While a lower common validation loss confirms that training optimized the intended objective, only closed-loop task acceptance, recovery resilience, and load-tested serving configurations provide defensible release evidence. A formal release approval specifies the exact artifact hash, its certified operating envelope, bounded residual uncertainties, and phased rollout criteria under principle 12. Even after a fine-tuned model passes release qualification, external safety boundaries remain non-negotiable: runtime schema validators, permission sandboxes, token budgets, and supervisory recovery monitors must remain fully active in production.

15.12 Case Study: Training the Repair Policy

The recovery record that opened the chapter can now become a training artifact. The behavior to teach is narrow. When the runtime refuses an invalid tool proposal, the policy should inspect the schema, issue a corrected invocation that satisfies the interface, and end with a bounded report. Consider an illustrative supervised fine-tuning run that ingests repair_demonstrations_v1 to produce candidate policy trained_policy_v1. That artifact is a set of weights, distinct from ws_r20, the workspace the demonstration produced.

Each of the three adaptation choices is settled by evidence the chapter has already developed. The objective is imitation because the record supplies a demonstrated correction, not an admissible comparison; e2 and e4 were produced under different information, so they cannot form a preference pair (section 15.8). The behavior source is the admitted recovery corpus. It covers the malformed-argument refusal the recording happened to contain, not the version conflict that section 15.6 showed a policy trained on it would meet, so that uncovered state is the first candidate for a labeling round or a fault-injected record, and the first evaluation of trained_policy_v1 must include trials that reach it. The artifact is a full-parameter update, chosen for two reasons the chapter has developed. The repair policy is a single role, so none of the multi-role sharing that makes adapters attractive applies yet. The lesson is also a new recovery behavior, the kind for which a small rank is not known in advance to suffice, so the full update serves as the reference that any later adapter candidate must match. The choice has a price. On the hypothetical model of notebook 15.5, it keeps 128 GB of parameter state where an adapter run would keep 16.13 GB. A rank sweep whose adapter matches the full update’s paired acceptance, or a second role that must share the base, would flip the decision toward the adapter, which would then be a different candidate with its own evidence. Changing any one of the three choices produces a configuration that needs its own evidentiary trail.

Table 12 details the formal manifest bindings for this candidate policy. In production workflows, recording version identifiers alone is insufficient; a complete training record must capture the exact artifact contents, serialization parameters, and optimization settings required to reproduce and audit the resulting model.

The mask and the serialization follow directly from the earlier sections. target_mask_v1 selects the policy-response spans of e3 (the schema query), e4 (the corrected tool call), and e6 (the final report), each with its authentic end-of-turn token. The malformed proposal and its pre-dispatch refusal at e2, the schema returned to e3, and the check summary at e5 carry \(\PositionLossMask_{t}=0\); they stay in the prefix as conditioning and receive no direct loss (table 2). Selecting e2 would teach the malformed call, and selecting the returned schema or e5 would teach the policy to write tool results instead of requesting them. Whether serialization_v1 may use one concatenated transcript or must serialize each selected invocation separately depends on whether the runtime compacted history between e2 and e6. If it did not, each selected response’s prefix is a prefix of one transcript and a single example is faithful. The manifest records which case held, from the delivered-input manifests, rather than assuming it, because a record that crossed the watermark would need one example per invocation at up to \((\TurnHorizon+1)/2\) the stored tokens (section 15.3.3). If the run packs these examples with others, block-diagonal attention and per-example label shifts keep each example’s context its own (section 15.5).

The release gate for trained_policy_v1 follows from the lesson it was meant to learn. Lower held-out loss on the repair corpus shows only that the objective was optimized. Promotion needs budgeted task acceptance on held-out repair tasks under the same runtime and fixtures as the incumbent, compared as paired trials, with \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\) reported for both so that a slower but more reliable candidate is credited correctly. Because the lesson is “consult the delivered schema,” the most direct test is schema-following accuracy and parametric recall error on a renamed-argument set: a candidate whose PRE rises there may be relying on the recorded interface rather than the delivered schema. The gate also needs trials that reach the version-conflict state the corpus does not cover, and a retention suite for the agent’s other required tasks. A candidate missing any of these measurements is held, however its loss compares (section 15.11).

Even when the evaluations justify a staged release, the proposal boundary stays where it was. Fine-tuning makes the policy’s proposals more reliable without making the model an execution authority. The runtime keeps enforcing schema validation, least-privilege tool permissions, process isolation, and hard timeout budgets, and the agent’s final report remains a claim the harness checks against workspace artifacts. The manifest is what turns one admitted recovery into a reproducible, auditable policy update; the runtime is what keeps that update’s mistakes bounded.

15.13 Fallacies and Pitfalls

A fine-tuning pipeline can optimize its nominal loss objective with complete numerical fidelity while departing from the intended operational behavior. In agent systems, where training corpora interweave multi-turn plans, tool invocations, and environment feedback, subtle flaws emerge at the interfaces between admitted evidence, context assembly, loss masking, and deployment evaluation. Each of the following mistakes produces a concrete failure mode, and each carries a quantifiable engineering penalty illustrated in this chapter’s worked examples.

Fallacy: Every model response in an accepted trajectory is a desirable training target.

When an agent successfully recovers from an error, the recorded trajectory necessarily contains the initial flawed or refused proposal alongside the eventual correction. That initial misstep is essential as conditioning context, because it explains why the correction was required, but it is the one action the policy must never learn to emit. In the inventory of notebook 15.1, the refused positions number 160 tokens, 20 percent as many as the 800 selected response targets. An unselective mask over all assistant turns therefore puts 160 of the 960 response positions to work reproducing the malformed call. That share is a minority of the response weight, yet all of it rewards the refused proposal directly, in every recovery record that contains one. Systems must instead implement target admission masks (section 15.4): retain rejected proposals and environment observations in the prefix context so the agent learns to condition on failure signals, but restrict direct imitation loss to admitted response targets.

Pitfall: Treating efficient batching as a change with no learning consequences.

Packing multiple variable-length trajectories into a single contiguous buffer raises occupancy, but it alters training dynamics unless the execution kernel accounts for sequence boundaries. In notebook 15.2, sequence packing raises token occupancy from 19.1 percent to 76.6 percent. However, applying a standard causal attention mask across that packed buffer allows subsequent examples to attend across episode boundaries to unrelated earlier sequences. Loss normalization alters optimization just as profoundly: standard token-level averaging gives the long example 32× the short one’s weight, whereas per-example averaging weights both examples equally. Always configure block-diagonal attention with per-example position coordinates and reset label shifts (section 15.5), explicitly declare loss reduction semantics, and verify real execution throughput on target accelerator kernels before claiming a speedup.

Fallacy: Recovery examples and schema perturbations make a fine-tuned agent robust to the states it will meet.

Synthesized recovery trajectories and perturbed schemas broaden training coverage, but they do not provide a blanket guarantee against distribution shift. Theoretical bounds on policy regret depend fundamentally on where state error rates are evaluated. Over an execution horizon of 30 steps with a 2 percent per-step disagreement rate, theorem 15.1 bounds excess cost at 18 units on reference demonstration states, but at 0.6 units on states generated by the learner itself. Furthermore, that tighter bound holds only while the disadvantage of each individual deviation remains near one, a condition that fails as soon as one unexpected tool error can spoil the rest of the task. Robust agent systems require sampling and labeling states actually reached by the learner, maintaining end-to-end coherence between schema transformations, tool calls, and parser observations (section 15.7), and stress-testing the policy under held-out operational shifts.

Pitfall: Reading a preference pair’s logistic score as the probability that the preferred repair succeeds.

The implicit reward margin computed in direct preference optimization reflects pairwise discrimination on historical data, not absolute operational success. In notebook 15.4, identical trajectory log-probabilities yield a logistic score of 0.646 at \(\PreferenceScale=\) 0.2, yet that score jumps to 0.769 simply by doubling the preference scale to \(\PreferenceScale=\) 0.4. A metric that shifts this much with an optimization hyperparameter measures relative fitting margin rather than task competence. Furthermore, that margin can widen because the objective suppresses the dispreferred trajectory rather than improving the execution likelihood of the preferred one. Reliable acceptance rates cannot be read off training curves; they must be measured directly through closed-loop environment trials (section 15.11).

Fallacy: Training a small fraction of the parameters shrinks training memory in proportion.

Parameter-efficient fine-tuning cuts the number of updated weights to a small fraction, but accelerator memory does not scale down linearly with the parameter fraction. In notebook 15.5, low-rank adapters account for 0.10 percent of total model parameters, yet the training job still requires 12.6 percent of the parameter-associated memory consumed by a full fine-tuning run. This 7.9× reduction is governed by bytes per parameter across base and adapter states rather than the parameter count alone. The frozen base weights must remain resident in memory throughout forward and backward passes, accounting for 99.2 percent of what remains, while dynamic activation buffers and workspace memory scale with sequence length for both approaches. Dimension adapter training hardware by accounting for frozen base representations and empirically measured activation peaks (section 15.10).

Pitfall: Promoting the artifact with the lowest recorded loss.

Selecting an agent checkpoint based purely on validation loss conflates prefix prediction with closed-loop execution competence. In notebook 15.1, an unmasked causal evaluation averages across 16,383 prediction terms per example. Tool outputs that the policy never emits occupy 77.1 percent of the stored input. In contrast, a selective objective evaluates only the 800 target positions where the model generates actions. A checkpoint with lower overall loss may simply fit the repetitive parts of that environment text more tightly without improving decision quality. Even when measured exclusively on action tokens, offline likelihood improvements do not guarantee that the policy will avoid deadlocks or handle schema variations during execution. Always qualify production candidates by measuring scoped task acceptance, effective cost \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), schema compliance, and retained foundational capabilities (section 15.11), while maintaining strict runtime validation checks regardless of training performance.

15.14 Summary

Trajectory fine-tuning changes the policy itself, and every decision in this chapter serves one requirement, that the update teach the lesson curation admitted and nothing else. A trajectory mixes the policy’s responses with the environment’s replies, so a loss computed over the wrong positions, under the wrong prefixes, or with the wrong batch weighting can fall while the behavior it teaches drifts. Even a faithful update is fitted on recorded prefixes. Its value is settled only by closed-loop evidence on the states the policy reaches on its own, under the same runtime that bounds every proposal it makes.

The quantitative decisions of the chapter rest on the relations collected in table 13.

Key Takeaways: Preserve the lesson from record to release
  • Weights hold procedure, never contracts: Training changes what the policy proposes, not what the runtime permits. Stable procedure can move into the weights, while schemas, permissions, and any guidance that changes faster than a training and evaluation cycle stay in context, and enforcement stays in the runtime.
  • A trajectory has two authors; train one: Observations stay in the input as context but receive no direct loss. An unmasked average dilutes each selected target by the inverse of its selected share of predicted positions, 20.5× in the worked inventory, and teaches the policy to invent tool results.
  • A loader can change the lesson without changing the loss: Later evidence in a prefix, a shifted label, or a causal mask across packed examples each redefines the prediction problem. Normalization is a choice too, since token averaging gives a long trajectory 32× the weight of a short one in the worked batch.
  • Where error is measured sets the horizon penalty: The same 2 percent disagreement over 30 steps bounds excess cost at 18 units on reference states and 0.6 on learner states when the deviation disadvantage is bounded by one, so long-horizon agents need labels on the states they actually visit.
  • A preference score measures one comparison: A pair’s logistic score moves with \(\PreferenceScale\) and can rise by suppressing the dispreferred record, so it measures fit to that comparison, never the probability that a repair is accepted.
  • Adapters cut state by bytes, not parameter fraction: Training 0.10 percent of the parameters still keeps 12.6 percent of the parameter state, because the frozen base stays resident. The lever is the frozen base’s representation, not the adapter rank.
  • Release on closed-loop acceptance, not loss: A candidate is promoted on task acceptance, \(\CostPerAcceptedTask\), schema behavior, and retained capability under its full serving configuration. Rollback selects an earlier policy for future work and leaves effects already dispatched to settlement and compensation.

Verified trajectory post-training (principle 13) now has a concrete implementation obligation at the token level: preserve useful conditioning while aligning the objective with the selected predictions, rather than equating every recorded policy token with a target. At the record level, it connects the admitted lesson to a versioned update whose usefulness remains an evaluation question. Invariant closure (principle 1) stays outside that update, which is why the proposal boundary holds from the first training example to the release decision.

15.15 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

The core mechanism of this chapter is older than language models. Williams and Zipser named teacher forcing in 1989, training recurrent networks by feeding the recorded target back as the next input (Williams and Zipser 1989). At about the same time, ALVINN learned road following as a supervised mapping from camera images to a steering direction (Pomerleau 1988), the template for what control researchers came to call behavioral cloning. Both generations met the same obstacle. A policy trained on recorded prefixes is run on its own, and its first error moves it into inputs the recording never showed. Ross and Bagnell turned that obstacle into a quadratic bound in 2010 (Ross and Bagnell 2010), and DAgger answered it the following year by labeling the states the learner itself visits (Ross et al. 2011). Sequence-generation research reached the same diagnosis independently, with scheduled sampling (Bengio et al. 2015) and the name exposure bias (Ranzato et al. 2016).

Williams, Ronald J., and David Zipser. 1989. “A Learning Algorithm for Continually Running Fully Recurrent Neural Networks.” Neural Computation 1 (2): 270–80. https://doi.org/10.1162/neco.1989.1.2.270.
Pomerleau, Dean A. 1988. “ALVINN: An Autonomous Land Vehicle in a Neural Network.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NIPS) 1: 305–13.
Bengio, Samy, Oriol Vinyals, Navdeep Jaitly, and Noam Shazeer. 2015. “Scheduled Sampling for Sequence Prediction with Recurrent Neural Networks.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2015) 28.
Bradley, Ralph Allan, and Milton E. Terry. 1952. “Rank Analysis of Incomplete Block Designs: I. The Method of Paired Comparisons.” Biometrika 39 (3/4): 324–45. https://doi.org/10.2307/2334029.
Christiano, P. F., J. Leike, T. B. Brown, M. Martic, S. Legg, and D. Amodei. 2017. “Deep Reinforcement Learning from Human Preferences.” In Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems 30: Annual Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems 2017, December 4-9, 2017, Long Beach, CA, USA, edited by Isabelle Guyon, Ulrike von Luxburg, Samy Bengio, Hanna M. Wallach, Rob Fergus, S. V. N. Vishwanathan, and Roman Garnett. Curran Associates.

Preference learning has a statistical root. Bradley and Terry’s 1952 model of paired comparisons (Bradley and Terry 1952) underlies the reward models that Christiano and colleagues fit to human comparisons of agent behavior (Christiano et al. 2017), and InstructGPT combined supervised demonstrations with such a reward model to align a language model (Ouyang et al. 2022). DPO then showed that the separate reward model and reinforcement learning stage could be folded into a classification loss on policy/reference log ratios (Rafailov et al. 2023), the form this chapter extends from responses to selected trajectory spans.

The cost of adaptation has its own lineage. Model compression (Buciluă et al. 2006) and knowledge distillation (Hinton et al. 2015) made a large model’s behavior a training signal for a cheaper one, and context distillation applied the same idea to a prompt (Askell et al. 2021). Adapter modules (Houlsby et al. 2019), low-rank adaptation (Hu et al. 2021), and quantized frozen bases (Dettmers et al. 2023) shrank what each adaptation stores and trains, while the systems accounting of mixed-precision training (Micikevicius et al. 2018) and ZeRO (Rajbhandari et al. 2020) made the per-parameter memory of that training explicit. Agent fine-tuning joined these threads once models emitted tool calls inside interleaved reasoning and action traces (Yao et al. 2023; Schick et al. 2023), and trajectories generated by stronger models became training data for smaller ones (Chen et al. 2023; Zeng et al. 2023). What changes for agents is the environment: a trajectory has two authors, which is why masking, serialization, and on-policy coverage become engineering obligations rather than details.

Buciluă, Cristian, Rich Caruana, and Alexandru Niculescu-Mizil. 2006. “Model Compression.” Proceedings of the 12th ACM SIGKDD International Conference on Knowledge Discovery and Data Mining (KDD), 535–41. https://doi.org/10.1145/1150402.1150464.
Hinton, Geoffrey, Oriol Vinyals, and Jeff Dean. 2015. “Distilling the Knowledge in a Neural Network.” arXiv Preprint.
Houlsby, Neil, Andrei Giurgiu, Stanislaw Jastrzebski, Bruna Morrone, Quentin de Laroussilhe, Andrea Gesmundo, Mohammad Attariyan, and Sylvain Gelly. 2019. “Parameter-Efficient Transfer Learning for NLP.” Proceedings of the 36th International Conference on Machine Learning (ICML), Proceedings of machine learning research, vol. 97: 2790–99.
Dettmers, Tim, Artidoro Pagnoni, Ari Holtzman, and Luke Zettlemoyer. 2023. “QLoRA: Efficient Finetuning of Quantized LLMs.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 10088–115.
Micikevicius, Paulius, Sharan Narang, Jonah Alben, Gregory Diamos, Erich Elsen, David Garcia, Boris Ginsburg, et al. 2018. “Mixed Precision Training.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Yao, Shunyu, Jeffrey Zhao, Dian Yu, Nan Du, Izhak Shafran, Karthik Narasimhan, and Yuan Cao. 2023. “ReAct: Synergizing Reasoning and Acting in Language Models.” International Conference on Learning Representations (ICLR).
Schick, Timo, Jane Dwivedi-Yu, Roberto Dessı̀, Roberta Raileanu, Maria Lomeli, Luke Zettlemoyer, Nicola Cancedda, and Thomas Scialom. 2023. “Toolformer: Language Models Can Teach Themselves to Use Tools.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 68539–51.
Chen, Baian, Chang Shu, Ehsan Shareghi, Nigel Collier, Karthik Narasimhan, and Shunyu Yao. 2023. “FireAct: Toward Language Agent Fine-Tuning.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.05915, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2310.05915.
Zeng, Aohan, Mingdao Liu, Rui Lu, Bowen Wang, Xiao Liu, Yuxiao Dong, and Jie Tang. 2023. “AgentTuning: Enabling Generalized Agent Capabilities for LLMs.” arXiv Preprint arXiv:2310.12823, ahead of print. https://doi.org/10.48550/arXiv.2310.12823.

For further reading:

  • Ross, Gordon, and Bagnell’s DAgger paper (Ross et al. 2011) states both regret bounds of theorem 15.1 and the reduction to online learning behind them; it is the shortest route to seeing why where error is measured matters more than how small it is.
  • Rafailov and colleagues’ DPO paper (Rafailov et al. 2023) derives the preference loss from a reference-regularized objective, and reading the derivation shows exactly which assumptions a trajectory-span surrogate relaxes.
  • Ouyang and colleagues’ InstructGPT paper (Ouyang et al. 2022) documents a complete demonstration-then-preference pipeline, including the human evaluations that compared the model after each stage.
  • Hu and colleagues’ LoRA paper (Hu et al. 2021) motivates low-rank updates and reports experiments on which matrices to adapt and at what rank, the knobs this chapter says to set by evaluation.
  • Rajbhandari and colleagues’ ZeRO paper (Rajbhandari et al. 2020) itemizes training memory by owner and precision, the same inventory discipline notebook 15.5 applies to an adapter run.
  • Krell and colleagues’ packing paper (Krell et al. 2021) treats packing as an equivalence to prove rather than a throughput trick, the stance the chapter’s packed-versus-separate logit test takes.
Ross, Stéphane, Geoffrey Gordon, and J. Andrew Bagnell. 2011. “A Reduction of Imitation Learning and Structured Prediction to No-Regret Online Learning.” Proceedings of the Fourteenth International Conference on Artificial Intelligence and Statistics (AISTATS) 15: 627–35.
Rafailov, Rafael, Archit Sharma, Eric Mitchell, Stefano Ermon, Christopher D. Manning, and Chelsea Finn. 2023. “Direct Preference Optimization: Your Language Model Is Secretly a Reward Model.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 36: 53728–41. https://doi.org/10.52202/075280-2338.
Ouyang, L., J. Wu, X. Jiang, D. Almeida, C. L. Wainwright, P. Mishkin, C. Zhang, et al. 2022. “Training Language Models to Follow Instructions with Human Feedback.” Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS) 35: 27730–44. https://doi.org/10.52202/068431-2011.
